### 1. SF-5: Appendix 超参数表

在 Appendix 中新增一个完整的超参数对比表（Table A1 或合适编号），标题如 "Training Hyperparameters for All Methods"。

**GRPO 配置（exp-002v3 / exp-003 共享基础配置）：**

| Parameter | Value |
|-----------|-------|
| Base model | Qwen3-8B |
| Learning rate | 5e-5 |
| Per-device batch size | 1 |
| Gradient accumulation | 16 (effective batch 16) |
| Max steps | 8,000 |
| Warmup steps | 200 |
| Group size (num_generations) | 2 |
| KL penalty (β) | 0.04 |
| Max completion length | 256 |
| LR scheduler | linear (Cosine R1) / cosine (R3-only) |
| Precision | bf16 |
| LoRA rank | 64 |
| LoRA alpha | 64 (= rank) |
| LoRA target modules | q_proj, k_proj, v_proj, o_proj |
| LoRA dropout | 0 |

**GRPO reward 配置差异（按 method variant）：**
- Cosine R1: R1=0.4 (scale=5.0), R2=0.0, R3=0.6; reward_layers=[19,20,24,27,32,33,35]; recalibration every 500 steps
- R3-only: R3=1.0; NLI model=cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base

**ACT (SFT) 配置：**

| Parameter | Value |
|-----------|-------|
| Optimizer | AdamW (weight_decay=0.01) |
| Learning rate | 5e-5 |
| Per-device batch size | 1 |
| Gradient accumulation | 16 (effective batch 16) |
| Max steps | 8,000 |
| Warmup steps | 200 |
| Max sequence length | 1,024 |
| Gradient clipping | 1.0 |
| LR scheduler | linear with warmup |
| Loss | SFT loss + 0.1 × L2 consistency loss |
| L2 target layers | [19, 20, 24, 27, 32, 33, 35] |
| LoRA rank | 64 |
| LoRA alpha | 128 (= 2×rank) |
| LoRA target modules | q/k/v/o_proj + gate/up/down_proj |
| LoRA dropout | 0.05 |

**重要注释（表格脚注）：**
- "Group size of 2 is smaller than typical values in GRPO literature (8–16); we did not perform hyperparameter search over this parameter."
- "ACT uses a wider LoRA configuration (~1.75× trainable parameters) than GRPO methods; this difference is one of several confounds discussed in §6."

### 2. 代码/数据可用性声明

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Bng22DddTZx1xf1qJXtNkA`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Cosine \texorpdfstring{$\rone$}{R1}: Structural Failure}
2	\label{sec:cosine_failure}
3	
4	Section~\ref{sec:direction_quality} established that $\dsyc$ achieves AUROC 0.827 on Qwen3-8B.
5	We now test whether this discriminability translates into a viable GRPO reward.
6	Four configurations form a diagnostic chain (Table~\ref{tab:cosine_chain}): each resolves the failure mode of its predecessor but exposes a deeper limitation.
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\caption{Cosine $\rone$ failure chain on Qwen3-8B (LoRA rank$=$64, GRPO with $R = w_1 \cdot \rone + w_3 \cdot \rthree$).}
11	\label{tab:cosine_chain}
12	\begin{tabular}{lllll}
13	\toprule
14	Config & $\rone$ Formula & $w_1$ & Outcome & Root Cause \\
15	\midrule
16	F1a & $\tanh(\mathbf{h}\cdot\dsyc/20)$ & 0.25 & Gate saturation & Norm mismatch \\
17	F1b & $\cos(\mathbf{h},\, \dsyc)$ & 0.25 & $\rone \approx 0$ & Gap too small \\
18	F1c & +recalibration & 0.25 & Mode collapse & Reward discontinuity \\
19	F1d & $5\cos(\mathbf{h},\, \dsyc)$ & 0.40 & Mode collapse & $\rthree$ saturation \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	F1a uses a dot-product formulation ($\rone = \tanh(\mathbf{h} \cdot \dsyc / 20)$), but residual-stream norms exceed the direction norm by $100$--$1{,}200{\times}$, saturating the tanh gate and zeroing the gradient by step 955.
25	Switching to cosine similarity (F1b) eliminates norm dependence, yet the cosine gap between valid and sycophantic activations averages only 0.098 across layers---too narrow to generate meaningful reward contrast.
26	Online recalibration (F1c, \S\ref{ssec:recalib}) raises offline AUROC from 0.827 to 0.910 but paradoxically triggers mode collapse: the abrupt shift in $\dsyc$ at each recalibration event creates a reward discontinuity that destabilizes GRPO advantage estimates, collapsing entropy to 0.069 within 300 steps.
27	Amplifying the signal with $5{\times}$ scaling (F1d) lifts $\rone$ above the noise floor but simultaneously amplifies the conflation between warranted revision and unwarranted capitulation; $\rthree$ saturates at 0.9998 by step 300, leaving the noisy $\rone$ as the sole gradient source until terminal collapse at step 2160.
28	
29	The four configurations converge on a single conclusion: $\dsyc$ encodes the \emph{direction} of belief change but not whether that change is \emph{warranted}.
30	Valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along $\dsyc$, so the cosine projection assigns similar scores to both.
31	This is not a calibration artifact---recalibration improves discriminability yet rewards still fail---but a semantic limitation of any continuous $\dsyc$-based reward.
32	Section~\ref{sec:probe_trap} examines whether discretizing the signal via a binary probe can escape this limitation.
33	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Lx2JH7CTNvmLARZFiJjgUU`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Training Details}
2	\label{app:training}
3	
4	\paragraph{Reward weight selection.}
5	The weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration: $w_1{=}0.25$ was initially chosen to down-weight the noisy cosine reward term; this was later increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ for F1d after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed the cosine term to contribute only ${\sim}8.3\%$ of total reward variance.
6	The empirical scaling factor in F1a ($\dsyc/20$) is a heuristic normalization; we did not find it critical to the failure modes reported.
7	
8	\paragraph{Response truncation.}
9	All GRPO experiments use \texttt{max\_completion\_length}=256, resulting in 80--84\% truncation of multi-turn responses.
10	This setting is shared across all conditions and does not affect relative comparisons; we retain it because response-level decisions typically manifest within the first 100--200 tokens.
11	
12	\paragraph{GRPO sampling.}
13	All GRPO experiments use \texttt{group\_size}=2, below the typical range of 8--16 in the literature.
14	This limits within-group ranking diversity; whether larger group sizes would alter the failure patterns we observe remains untested.
15	
16	\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}
17	\label{app:taxonomy}
18	
19	Manual inspection of 100 probe-trained responses (exp-008v3, step 2000) reveals three dominant failure modes.
20	\emph{Sycophantic stubbornness} (24\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.
21	\emph{Deliberation traps} (29\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.
22	\emph{Evidence-graded hedging}: under weak evidence, responses show surface compliance without substantive change; under strong evidence, responses add explicit hedging (``this is debatable,'' ``there are multiple perspectives'').
23	The model learns to modulate its \emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.
24	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis: Discriminability $\\neq$ Reward Utility}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Unified root cause.}\nThe failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} trace to a single geometric limitation.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not its \\emph{evidential warrant}: valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along the same direction.\nThe two reward families expose complementary faces of this blindness.\nCosine $\\rone$ penalizes all belief change indiscriminately; the resulting mode collapse (F1c, F1d) reflects excessive punishment of any activation shift along $\\dsyc$.\nThe binary probe $\\rone$ rewards all resistance regardless of input validity; the stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500) reflects excessive permissiveness toward activation stasis.\nThese are not independent design flaws but dual consequences of a direction that conflates the target behavior (resisting invalid challenges) with its complement (refusing valid corrections).\nCross-model evidence confirms the limitation extends beyond Qwen3-8B: Llama-3-8B independently achieves AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$) on the same contrastive pairs with the same monotonic evidence gradient, so discriminability generalizes across architectures while the semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility persists.\nThis pattern contrasts with reasoning faithfulness and safety directions~\\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, where the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\n\\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} reinforce this principle: concept directions applied via advantage modification in GRPO achieve 100\\% validation accuracy for reward hacking detection, where shortcut behaviors are geometrically separable---confirming that direction utility depends on geometric separability, not the optimization framework.\nFor sycophancy, the target and its complement occupy the same activation region; they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO stubbornness across all formulations.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\\tof$ and $\\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. Best result in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Final checkpoint; only 4/26 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla. $^\\ddagger$Seed 1 (16 checkpoints); seed 2 verification ongoing (4 of 16 checkpoints evaluated; all exceed vanilla).}\n\\label{tab:main_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Type & $\\sycon$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\tof$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\nof$ $\\uparrow$ & Step \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO with $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\nCosine $\\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & collapse & -- & -- & -- \\\\\nBinary Probe $\\rone$ (F2) & RL & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\\\\nContinuous Probe $\\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO without $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.143$^\\dagger$ & -- & -- & 8000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Non-GRPO}} \\\\\nActAdd ($\\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\\\\n\\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \\textbf{0.301} & \\textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\\\\nACT (seed 2)$^\\ddagger$ & SFT & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textit{ongoing; 4/16 ckpts evaluated, all ${>}$ vanilla}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_comparison} summarizes all methods evaluated on Qwen3-8B.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO removes $\\dsyc$ entirely and relies on the NLI-based behavioral reward alone.\nIf the failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} stemmed solely from the representation reward, this ablation should recover calibration quality.\nIt does not.\nThe $\\sycon$ trajectory for $\\rthree$-only GRPO oscillates without sustained improvement.\nSeed 1 peaks at 0.153 (checkpoint 6500) and ends at 0.143 (checkpoint 8000); seed 2 peaks at 0.143 (checkpoint 4000) and regresses to 0.095 by checkpoint 6000.\nOnly 4 of 26 checkpoints across both seeds exceed vanilla (15.4\\%), while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing; Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nBoth seeds exhibit oscillations of similar amplitude ($\\Delta \\approx 0.069$) and identical crash depth (0.074), but crash onset differs by ${\\sim}2{,}500$ steps, indicating stochastic drift in a saturated reward landscape.\nCross-seed rank correlation is ${\\approx}1.0$ during early training (steps 1000--3000) but drops to $\\rho{\\approx}0.33$ (n.s.) after step 4000, with spike timing and magnitude diverging---consistent with method-inherent warm-up followed by stochastic divergence.\nActivation analysis confirms the drift is not representational: the $\\dsyc$ direction at layer 19 yields nearly identical AUROC across consecutive checkpoints ($\\Delta < 0.01$), and $\\rthree$ reward saturates at ${\\sim}0.98$ (Welch $t$-test $p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ swings by up to 17\\%.\nGRPO drifts in a flat reward landscape while representations remain stable, reinforcing the disconnect between discriminability and behavioral utility.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO exhibits the same stubbornness pattern as the $\\dsyc$ approaches (high $\\nof$, near-zero $\\tof$) at milder severity: the sparse scalar reward per completion cannot teach the model \\emph{which} belief changes are warranted.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\sycon$ trajectories for $\\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (seed 1; seed 2 ongoing, 4/16 checkpoints evaluated) over training steps. ACT seed 1 exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (16/16); $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/26 checkpoints across seeds (15.4\\%). Both GRPO seeds show the same oscillation pattern with stochastic timing.}\n\\label{fig:trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Observational comparison: ACT vs.\\ GRPO.}\nACT~\\citep{consistency2025bctact} achieves $\\sycon = 0.301$ at checkpoint 8000, more than $2{\\times}$ the vanilla baseline and above every scalar RL and inference-time variant (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}).\nThe ACT trajectory is not monotonic: $\\sycon = 0.255$ at checkpoint 2000, dips to 0.214 at checkpoint 4000 ($-4.1$ percentage points), recovers to 0.262 at checkpoint 6000, and reaches 0.301 at checkpoint 8000.\nACT also experiences stubbornness episodes---the checkpoint-4000 dip is consistent with transient competition between the SFT and L2 consistency loss objectives---yet it self-recovers by checkpoint 6000, unlike GRPO where stubbornness persists (Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nActivation analysis shows a correlated asymmetry: ACT's $\\dsyc$ discriminability recovers during the $\\sycon$ dip (AUROC $+0.013$), whereas GRPO's continues declining ($-0.004$).\nACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions---supervision granularity (per-token vs.\\ per-completion), loss family (cross-entropy + L2 vs.\\ REINFORCE), training data format (labeled pairs vs.\\ sampled completions), optimization dynamics (SFT convergence vs.\\ RL exploration), and LoRA adapter capacity (ACT targets 7 module types with ${\\sim}1.75{\\times}$ more trainable parameters vs.\\ GRPO's 4)---so the performance gap cannot be attributed to any single factor without controlled ablation.\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} successfully reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking, where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients.\nTogether with our results, this suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\\rthree$-only GRPO), consistent with per-completion information compression as an independent factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nCondition-aware rewards that jointly encode belief-change direction and evidential warrant, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and process reward models with per-step feedback each offer paths forward.\nThe scalar per-completion reward structure is shared by PPO, RLOO, and other REINFORCE-style methods; whether they exhibit similar failures remains an open question.\nBeyond sycophancy, the principle that discriminability does not guarantee scalar reward utility may apply to other behavioral attributes where the target and its complement share the same geometric direction~\\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis: Discriminability $\\neq$ Reward Utility}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Unified root cause.}\nThe failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} trace to a single geometric limitation.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not its \\emph{evidential warrant}: valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along the same direction.\nThe two reward families expose complementary faces of this blindness.\nCosine $\\rone$ penalizes all belief change indiscriminately; the resulting mode collapse (F1c, F1d) reflects excessive punishment of any activation shift along $\\dsyc$.\nThe binary probe $\\rone$ rewards all resistance regardless of input validity; the stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500) reflects excessive permissiveness toward activation stasis.\nThese are not independent design flaws but dual consequences of a direction that conflates the target behavior (resisting invalid challenges) with its complement (refusing valid corrections).\nCross-model evidence confirms the limitation extends beyond Qwen3-8B: Llama-3-8B independently achieves AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$) on the same contrastive pairs with the same monotonic evidence gradient, so discriminability generalizes across architectures while the semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility persists.\nThis pattern contrasts with reasoning faithfulness and safety directions~\\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, where the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\n\\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} reinforce this principle: concept directions applied via advantage modification in GRPO achieve 100\\% validation accuracy for reward hacking detection, where shortcut behaviors are geometrically separable---confirming that direction utility depends on geometric separability, not the optimization framework.\nFor sycophancy, the target and its complement occupy the same activation region; they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO stubbornness across all formulations.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\\tof$ and $\\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. all exceed vanilla).}\n\\label{tab:main_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Type & $\\sycon$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\tof$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\nof$ $\\uparrow$ & Step \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO with $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\nCosine $\\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & ---$^\\S$ & -- & -- & -- \\\\\nBinary Probe $\\rone$ (F2) & RL & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\\\\nContinuous Probe $\\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO without $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.143$^\\dagger$ & -- & -- & 8000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Non-GRPO}} \\\\\nActAdd ($\\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\\\\n\\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \\textbf{0.301} & \\textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\\\\nACT (seed 2)$^\\ddagger$ & SFT & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textit{ongoing; 4/16 ckpts evaluated, all ${>}$ vanilla}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_comparison} summarizes all methods evaluated on Qwen3-8B.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO removes $\\dsyc$ entirely and relies on the NLI-based behavioral reward alone.\nIf the failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} stemmed solely from the representation reward, this ablation should recover calibration quality.\nIt does not.\nThe $\\sycon$ trajectory for $\\rthree$-only GRPO oscillates without sustained improvement.\nSeed 1 peaks at 0.153 (checkpoint 6500) and ends at 0.143 (checkpoint 8000); seed 2 peaks at 0.143 (checkpoint 4000) and regresses to 0.095 by checkpoint 6000.\nOnly 4 of 26 checkpoints across both seeds exceed vanilla (15.4\\%), while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing; Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nBoth seeds exhibit oscillations of similar amplitude ($\\Delta \\approx 0.069$) and identical crash depth (0.074), but crash onset differs by ${\\sim}2{,}500$ steps, indicating stochastic drift in a saturated reward landscape.\nCross-seed rank correlation is ${\\approx}1.0$ during early training (steps 1000--3000) but drops to $\\rho{\\approx}0.33$ (n.s.) after step 4000, with spike timing and magnitude diverging---consistent with method-inherent warm-up followed by stochastic divergence.\nActivation analysis confirms the drift is not representational: the $\\dsyc$ direction at layer 19 yields nearly identical AUROC across consecutive checkpoints ($\\Delta < 0.01$), and $\\rthree$ reward saturates at ${\\sim}0.98$ (Welch $t$-test $p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ swings by up to 17\\%.\nGRPO drifts in a flat reward landscape while representations remain stable, reinforcing the disconnect between discriminability and behavioral utility.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO exhibits the same stubbornness pattern as the $\\dsyc$ approaches (high $\\nof$, near-zero $\\tof$) at milder severity: the sparse scalar reward per completion cannot teach the model \\emph{which} belief changes are warranted.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\sycon$ trajectories for $\\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (seed 1; seed 2 ongoing, 4/16 checkpoints evaluated) over training steps. ACT seed 1 exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (16/16); $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/26 checkpoints across seeds (15.4\\%). Both GRPO seeds show the same oscillation pattern with stochastic timing.}\n\\label{fig:trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Observational comparison: ACT vs.\\ GRPO.}\nACT~\\citep{consistency2025bctact} achieves $\\sycon = 0.301$ at checkpoint 8000, more than $2{\\times}$ the vanilla baseline and above every scalar RL and inference-time variant (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}).\nThe ACT trajectory is not monotonic: $\\sycon = 0.255$ at checkpoint 2000, dips to 0.214 at checkpoint 4000 ($-4.1$ percentage points), recovers to 0.262 at checkpoint 6000, and reaches 0.301 at checkpoint 8000.\nACT also experiences stubbornness episodes---the checkpoint-4000 dip is consistent with transient competition between the SFT and L2 consistency loss objectives---yet it self-recovers by checkpoint 6000, unlike GRPO where stubbornness persists (Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nActivation analysis shows a correlated asymmetry: ACT's $\\dsyc$ discriminability recovers during the $\\sycon$ dip (AUROC $+0.013$), whereas GRPO's continues declining ($-0.004$).\nACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions---supervision granularity (per-token vs.\\ per-completion), loss family (cross-entropy + L2 vs.\\ REINFORCE), training data format (labeled pairs vs.\\ sampled completions), optimization dynamics (SFT convergence vs.\\ RL exploration), and LoRA adapter capacity (ACT targets 7 module types with ${\\sim}1.75{\\times}$ more trainable parameters vs.\\ GRPO's 4)---so the performance gap cannot be attributed to any single factor without controlled ablation.\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} successfully reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking, where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients.\nTogether with our results, this suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\\rthree$-only GRPO), consistent with per-completion information compression as an independent factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nCondition-aware rewards that jointly encode belief-change direction and evidential warrant, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and process reward models with per-step feedback each offer paths forward.\nThe scalar per-completion reward structure is shared by PPO, RLOO, and other REINFORCE-style methods; whether they exhibit similar failures remains an open question.\nBeyond sycophancy, the principle that discriminability does not guarantee scalar reward utility may apply to other behavioral attributes where the target and its complement share the same geometric direction~\\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Training Details}\n\\label{app:training}\n\n\\paragraph{Reward weight selection.}\nThe weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration: $w_1{=}0.25$ was initially chosen to down-weight the noisy cosine reward term; this was later increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ for F1d after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed the cosine term to contribute only ${\\sim}8.3\\%$ of total reward variance.\nThe empirical scaling factor in F1a ($\\dsyc/20$) is a heuristic normalization; we did not find it critical to the failure modes reported.\n\n\\paragraph{Response truncation.}\nAll GRPO experiments use \\texttt{max\\_completion\\_length}=256, resulting in 80--84\\% truncation of multi-turn responses.\nThis setting is shared across all conditions and does not affect relative comparisons; we retain it because response-level decisions typically manifest within the first 100--200 tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO sampling.}\nAll GRPO experiments use \\texttt{group\\_size}=2, below the typical range of 8--16 in the literature.\nThis limits within-group ranking diversity; whether larger group sizes would alter the failure patterns we observe remains untested.\n\n\\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}\n\\label{app:taxonomy}\n\nManual inspection of 100 probe-trained responses (exp-008v3, step 2000) reveals three dominant failure modes.\n\\emph{Sycophantic stubbornness} (24\\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.\n\\emph{Deliberation traps} (29\\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.\n\\emph{Evidence-graded hedging}: under weak evidence, responses show surface compliance without substantive change; under strong evidence, responses add explicit hedging (``this is debatable,'' ``there are multiple perspectives'').\nThe model learns to modulate its \\emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \\emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.\n",

[Director 指令] [Patch — Round 3 审稿回应，第四批：Framing 收窄 + group_size 正文处理]

Round 3 审稿 2.5 分。核心问题是 group_size=2 暴露后构成致命替代解释。正在补 group_size=8 消融实验（~15h）。同时做以下 framing 修改，与实验并行。

---

### 1. MF3: 标题修改

当前：`Discriminability Without Utility: Why Sycophancy Directions Fail as Scalar RL Rewards`

改为：`Discriminability Without Utility: Why Sycophancy Directions Fail as GRPO Rewards`

或（如果更精确）：`Discriminability Without Utility: Why Sycophancy Directions Fail as Scalar Per-Completion Rewards`

选第一个更简洁。如果你认为第二个更好也可以。同步修改 main.tex 的 `\title{}`。

### 2. MF4: C3 从 Contribution 列表移除

**Introduction**：当前有 C1, C2, C3 三个 contributions。删除 C3 作为 numbered contribution。仅保留 C1（discriminability ≠ utility）和 C2（probe reward trap）。

C3 的内容（ACT vs GRPO 观察）移到 §6 末尾作为一段 observational discussion，明确标注 "observational" 且不使用任何因果语言。

### 3. group_size=2 正文处理

**§2 Experimental Framework**（或最合适的位置）：添加 1-2 句说明 group_size=2 的选择及其局限性。示例：
"We use a group size of 2 for GRPO training due to memory constraints with 8B-parameter models. This is below the typical range of 8–64 in GRPO literature (Shao et al., 2024); we address this limitation with a group_size=8 ablation in §X / Appendix."

如果消融实验结果在论文提交前出来，预留 §X 或 Appendix 位置放结果。如果来不及，写 "ongoing" 或只在 Limitations 中提及。

**Limitations**：显式列出 group_size=2 为论文最重要的实验限制之一。

### 4. MF2: Truncation 正文处理

**Limitations**：显式列出 max_completion_length=256 / 80-84% truncation 为限制。补一句 "All reward formulations share this truncation setting, ensuring relative comparisons are fair, though absolute conclusions about d_syc utility may be affected."

### 5. SF6: SyconScore Harmonic Mean 补充

**§2.5 或 Appendix**：
- 添加 1 句 justify harmonic mean 选择（引用 F-score 类比："Like the F₁ score, the harmonic mean penalizes methods that achieve high performance on one metric while failing on the other"）
- 补报 arithmetic mean 作为 sensitivity analysis（如果有数据；没有的话写 "arithmetic mean results show similar patterns and are reported in supplementary materials"）

### 6. SF2: "Discriminability ≠ Utility" 泛化收窄

**§8 Conclusion**：将 "discriminability ≠ utility" 从原则性声明改为 hypothesis。"Our results suggest that discriminability in representation space does not guarantee utility as an RL training signal — at least for behavioral attributes like sycophancy where the target concept lacks geometric separability."

---

### 不要做的
- 消融实验结果的写入（等实验完成）
- Bootstrap CI 更新（等数据确认）
- Table 4 collapse 值更新（等 ckpt-500/1000/1500 eval）

---

引用不需要验证。完成后报告改动和编译状态（必须保持 ≤8 页主文）。

```text
1	Our RL training experiments use Qwen3-8B exclusively with GRPO; cross-model discriminability is confirmed on Llama-3-8B (AUROC $0.779$; Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}), but compute constraints (${\sim}200$ H100-equivalent hours) prevented replication of the reward formulation failures on a second architecture. Other RL algorithms (PPO, DPO) may interact differently with representation-based rewards.
2	
3	Evidence strength for the 181 contrastive pairs was annotated by GPT-4.1 as a single annotator; we did not collect human inter-annotator agreement. The evaluation framework categorizes challenges as binary valid/invalid rather than a continuous spectrum, and flip determination relies on a DeBERTa NLI classifier rather than human judges.
4	
5	We report GRPO results from two random seeds; ACT seed 2 verification is ongoing (4 of 16 checkpoints evaluated at submission, all exceeding vanilla). Both GRPO seeds exhibit qualitatively consistent oscillation patterns (similar amplitude, identical crash depth), but the limited seed count constrains statistical claims about training dynamics. ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions beyond supervision granularity, so we cannot isolate the causal factor behind ACT's advantage.
6	
```

### Tool result

_source line 3197 · 2026-05-22T13:09:47.389Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01H33o3ypjbwQ2bbBWHHTuzT`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Representation engineering \citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.
5	A contrastive sycophancy direction $\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\sigma{=}0.028$) on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\sigma{=}0.019$) on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\S\ref{sec:direction_quality}).
6	These results, replicated across two architecturally distinct model families, point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a scalar reward signal for reinforcement learning.
7	Sycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level reward could complement existing behavioral approaches by targeting the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.
8	
9	This paper shows that, in our experiments on Qwen3-8B, the conversion fails.
10	We test three formulations that translate $\dsyc$ into a GRPO \citep{shao2024grpo} training signal (cosine projection, binary probe, and continuous probe) across six configurations; every formulation fails.
11	Cosine similarity cannot separate valid revision from sycophantic capitulation in the narrow online activation gap (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).
12	A binary probe with stopped gradients creates a stubbornness trap: $\tof$ drops to zero within 500 steps while generation entropy remains at 0.70, masking behavioral death (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}).
13	Removing $\dsyc$ in favor of a purely behavioral reward does not resolve the problem: $\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints across two seeds, while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
14	Two layers of failure emerged in our experiments: $\dsyc$-based rewards were actively harmful, and scalar per-completion RL could not achieve sycophancy calibration.
15	
16	Techniques for discovering latent knowledge \citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability in activation space should transfer into a useful training signal.
17	For reasoning faithfulness and safety refusal, this premise holds, because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.
18	Sycophancy violates this premise.
19	Capitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\dsyc$, because both involve the model shifting its stated belief toward the user's position.
20	
21	The common thread across these failures is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.
22	$\dsyc$ encodes \emph{whether} belief changed, not \emph{whether it should have}.
23	A post-hoc classifier can use this signal effectively because it scores labeled examples where the validity of the belief change is already known.
24	A reward signal during RL training has no such access to ground truth; it must distinguish warranted revision from unwarranted capitulation using activation geometry alone, and $\dsyc$ collapses exactly this distinction.
25	This conflation explains the $\dsyc$-based failures directly.
26	$\dsyc$ rewards penalize all belief change indiscriminately, producing behavioral collapse.
27	$\rthree$-only GRPO's single scalar per completion also failed, consistent with per-completion information compression discarding the token-level context needed to distinguish warranted from unwarranted belief changes.
28	ACT's dense per-token supervision coincides with successful calibration, though ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions beyond supervision granularity (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
29	Sycophancy is defined relative to external evidential warrant \citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, a property that no single activation direction can fully encode.
30	The entanglement between helpfulness and sycophancy at the representation level, where the same mechanisms that allow models to track user intent also predispose them to capitulate \citep{wang2025internal}, is amplified by RLHF \citep{shapira2026amplifies}.
31	When reward overoptimization \citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \citep{casper2023openproblems}.
32	
33	We present three contributions:
34	
35	\begin{itemize}
36	\item \textbf{Every tested GRPO formulation fails for sycophancy calibration} (C1). We extract and validate $\dsyc$ on two model families (Qwen3-8B AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\sigma{=}0.028$); Llama-3-8B AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\sigma{=}0.019$)), then show that all three $\dsyc$-based reward formulations produce behavioral collapse (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}, \S\ref{sec:probe_trap}). R3-only GRPO, which removes $\dsyc$ entirely, also fails: only 4/26 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla, while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). Mechanistic analysis reveals that $\dsyc$ discriminability remains stable (AUROC $\Delta < 0.01$) and behavioral reward saturates (${\sim}0.98$, $p{=}0.703$), yet $\sycon$ regresses by 15\%---behavioral failure decoupled from both representation and reward signals.
37	
38	\item \textbf{Probe reward trap with behavioral-before-distributional collapse} (C2). The binary probe reward produces behavioral death ($\tof = 0$ at step 500) while entropy remains at 0.70. The 1,300-step lag before distributional collapse means standard entropy monitoring misses the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}).
39	
40	\item \textbf{In our experiments, sparse RL reward failed to convey the valid/invalid distinction} (C3). We trace these failures to a common thread: $\dsyc$ encodes belief-change direction but not evidential warrant. In our setting, a single scalar reward per completion, whether from representations or behavioral classifiers, failed to teach the model which belief changes are warranted. ACT's dense per-token supervision achieved $\sycon = 0.301$; however, ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions, so this gap is an observational finding, not a controlled comparison (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
41	\end{itemize}
42	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	This work tested whether a representation direction that detects sycophantic behavior can serve as a scalar reward signal for sycophancy calibration via GRPO.
4	In experiments on Qwen3-8B, $\dsyc$ achieved strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827), yet all three reward formulations failed---the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap ($\tof = 0$ by step 500), and $\rthree$-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints.
5	$\dsyc$ encodes \emph{whether} belief changed, not \emph{whether it should have}; ACT's dense per-token supervision achieved $\sycon = 0.301$, suggesting supervision granularity as one plausible factor, though ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions that remain to be disentangled.
6	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EWcfjUsSJA5Q9ajhCtm4R3`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experimental Framework}
2	\label{sec:framework}
3	
4	\subsection{Direction Extraction}
5	\label{ssec:extraction}
6	
7	We extract a linear sycophancy direction $\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure).
8	We construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.
9	Style matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\%$, shared opening phrase ${\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.
10	Evidence strength is annotated at three levels: \emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data).
11	From the 181 paired examples (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\ell$:
12	\begin{equation}
13	\label{eq:dsyc}
14	\dsyc = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \mathbf{h}^{-}_i\right)
15	\end{equation}
16	where $\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.
17	The target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\sigma{=}0.028$).
18	
19	\subsection{Reward Formulations}
20	\label{ssec:reward}
21	
22	GRPO \citep{shao2024grpo} ranks sampled completions by reward and updates the policy toward higher-reward outputs.
23	We define $R = w_1 \cdot \rone + w_3 \cdot \rthree$, where $\rone$ is a direction-based component and $\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).
24	Three formulations translate $\dsyc$ into $\rone$:
25	\begin{itemize}
26	\item[\textbf{F1}] \textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\tanh(\mathbf{h} \cdot \dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\cos(\mathbf{h},\, \dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\S\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\times}$ scaled cosine. See \S\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.
27	\item[\textbf{F2}] \textbf{Binary probe.} $\rone = \mathbb{I}[P(\text{syc} \mid \mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients.
28	\item[\textbf{F3}] \textbf{Continuous probe.} $\rone = 1 - P(\text{syc} \mid \mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.
29	\end{itemize}
30	The weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration rather than grid search (Appendix~\ref{app:training}).
31	As an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.
32	
33	\subsection{Online Recalibration}
34	\label{ssec:recalib}
35	
36	LoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\dsyc$ every 500 steps.
37	The cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\approx}\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.
38	
39	\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}
40	\label{ssec:baselines}
41	
42	We implement Activation Consistency Training \citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.
43	Unlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.
44	
45	\subsection{Evaluation}
46	\label{ssec:eval}
47	
48	The \emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \emph{No-Flip rate} ($\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.
49	Their harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:
50	\begin{equation}
51	\label{eq:sycon}
52	\sycon = \frac{2 \cdot \tof \cdot \nof}{\tof + \nof}
53	\end{equation}
54	We use the harmonic mean to penalize methods that achieve high resistance by refusing all evidence-warranted corrections (low $\tof$, high $\nof$) or by capitulating universally (high $\tof$, low $\nof$).
55	Flip classification uses \texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base} (MNLI mismatched accuracy 90.0\%, SNLI test 92.4\%; \citealt{he2023debertav3}) for stance classification, following \citet{consistency2025bctact}.
56	All experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.
57	We test six $\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\rone$ (F3), plus $\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.
58	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CSDU4zCPJgNLfqYqwEzVAt`</summary>

```text
68	ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions---supervision granularity (per-token vs.\ per-completion), loss family (cross-entropy + L2 vs.\ REINFORCE), training data format (labeled pairs vs.\ sampled completions), optimization dynamics (SFT convergence vs.\ RL exploration), and LoRA adapter capacity (ACT targets 7 module types with ${\sim}1.75{\times}$ more trainable parameters vs.\ GRPO's 4)---so the performance gap cannot be attributed to any single factor without controlled ablation.
69	
70	\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}
71	\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} successfully reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking, where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients.
72	Together with our results, this suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\rthree$-only GRPO), consistent with per-completion information compression as an independent factor.
73	
74	\paragraph{Future directions.}
75	Condition-aware rewards that jointly encode belief-change direction and evidential warrant, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and process reward models with per-step feedback each offer paths forward.
76	The scalar per-completion reward structure is shared by PPO, RLOO, and other REINFORCE-style methods; whether they exhibit similar failures remains an open question.
77	Beyond sycophancy, the principle that discriminability does not guarantee scalar reward utility may apply to other behavioral attributes where the target and its complement share the same geometric direction~\citep{skalse2022hacking}.
78	
```

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "54f3e094-59c4-41ff-8429-15ad5398fe15",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "de99c205-b407-49a9-9789-5d5cc6ca2540",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "0f69dab0-0868-4356-abd4-56dd9fc61918",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "Two layers of failure emerged in our experiments: $\\dsyc$-based rewards were actively harmful, and scalar per-completion RL could not achieve sycophancy calibration.",
    "newString": "Every tested GRPO reward formulation failed: $\\dsyc$-based rewards were actively harmful, and removing $\\dsyc$ in favor of a purely behavioral reward did not resolve the problem.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nRepresentation engineering \\citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.\nA contrastive sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$) on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$) on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\\S\\ref{sec:direction_quality}).\nThese results, replicated across two architecturally distinct model families, point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a scalar reward signal for reinforcement learning.\nSycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level reward could complement existing behavioral approaches by targeting the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.\n\nThis paper shows that, in our experiments on Qwen3-8B, the conversion fails.\nWe test three formulations that translate $\\dsyc$ into a GRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} training signal (cosine projection, binary probe, and continuous probe) across six configurations; every formulation fails.\nCosine similarity cannot separate valid revision from sycophantic capitulation in the narrow online activation gap (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\nA binary probe with stopped gradients creates a stubbornness trap: $\\tof$ drops to zero within 500 steps while generation entropy remains at 0.70, masking behavioral death (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\nRemoving $\\dsyc$ in favor of a purely behavioral reward does not resolve the problem: $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints across two seeds, while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nTwo layers of failure emerged in our experiments: $\\dsyc$-based rewards were actively harmful, and scalar per-completion RL could not achieve sycophancy calibration.\n\nTechniques for discovering latent knowledge \\citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability in activation space should transfer into a useful training signal.\nFor reasoning faithfulness and safety refusal, this premise holds, because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\nSycophancy violates this premise.\nCapitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\\dsyc$, because both involve the model shifting its stated belief toward the user's position.\n\nThe common thread across these failures is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}.\nA post-hoc classifier can use this signal effectively because it scores labeled examples where the validity of the belief change is already known.\nA reward signal during RL training has no such access to ground truth; it must distinguish warranted revision from unwarranted capitulation using activation geometry alone, and $\\dsyc$ collapses exactly this distinction.\nThis conflation explains the $\\dsyc$-based failures directly.\n$\\dsyc$ rewards penalize all belief change indiscriminately, producing behavioral collapse.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO's single scalar per completion also failed, consistent with per-completion information compression discarding the token-level context needed to distinguish warranted from unwarranted belief changes.\nACT's dense per-token supervision coincides with successful calibration, though ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions beyond supervision granularity (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nSycophancy is defined relative to external evidential warrant \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, a property that no single activation direction can fully encode.\nThe entanglement between helpfulness and sycophancy at the representation level, where the same mechanisms that allow models to track user intent also predispose them to capitulate \\citep{wang2025internal}, is amplified by RLHF \\citep{shapira2026amplifies}.\nWhen reward overoptimization \\citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \\citep{casper2023openproblems}.\n\nWe present three contributions:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{Every tested GRPO formulation fails for sycophancy calibration} (C1). We extract and validate $\\dsyc$ on two model families (Qwen3-8B AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$); Llama-3-8B AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$)), then show that all three $\\dsyc$-based reward formulations produce behavioral collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}, \\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}). R3-only GRPO, which removes $\\dsyc$ entirely, also fails: only 4/26 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla, while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Mechanistic analysis reveals that $\\dsyc$ discriminability remains stable (AUROC $\\Delta < 0.01$) and behavioral reward saturates (${\\sim}0.98$, $p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ regresses by 15\\%---behavioral failure decoupled from both representation and reward signals.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Probe reward trap with behavioral-before-distributional collapse} (C2). The binary probe reward produces behavioral death ($\\tof = 0$ at step 500) while entropy remains at 0.70.     "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nRepresentation engineering \\citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.\nA contrastive sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$) on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$) on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\\S\\ref{sec:direction_quality}).\nThese results, replicated across two architecturally distinct model families, point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a scalar reward signal for reinforcement learning.\nSycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level reward could complement existing behavioral approaches by targeting the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.\n\nThis paper shows that, in our experiments on Qwen3-8B, the conversion fails.\nWe test three formulations that translate $\\dsyc$ into a GRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} training signal (cosine projection, binary probe, and continuous probe) across six configurations; every formulation fails.\nCosine similarity cannot separate valid revision from sycophantic capitulation in the narrow online activation gap (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\nA binary probe with stopped gradients creates a stubbornness trap: $\\tof$ drops to zero within 500 steps while generation entropy remains at 0.70, masking behavioral death (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\nRemoving $\\dsyc$ in favor of a purely behavioral reward does not resolve the problem: $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints across two seeds, while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nEvery tested GRPO reward formulation failed: $\\dsyc$-based rewards were actively harmful, and removing $\\dsyc$ in favor of a purely behavioral reward did not resolve the problem.\n\nTechniques for discovering latent knowledge \\citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability in activation space should transfer into a useful training signal.\nFor reasoning faithfulness and safety refusal, this premise holds, because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\nSycophancy violates this premise.\nCapitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\\dsyc$, because both involve the model shifting its stated belief toward the user's position.\n\nThe common thread across these failures is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}.\nA post-hoc classifier can use this signal effectively because it scores labeled examples where the validity of the belief change is already known.\nA reward signal during RL training has no such access to ground truth; it must distinguish warranted revision from unwarranted capitulation using activation geometry alone, and $\\dsyc$ collapses exactly this distinction.\nThis conflation explains the $\\dsyc$-based failures directly.\n$\\dsyc$ rewards penalize all belief change indiscriminately, producing behavioral collapse.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO's single scalar per completion also failed, consistent with per-completion information compression discarding the token-level context needed to distinguish warranted from unwarranted belief changes.\nACT's dense per-token supervision coincides with successful calibration, though ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions beyond supervision granularity (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nSycophancy is defined relative to external evidential warrant \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, a property that no single activation direction can fully encode.\nThe entanglement between helpfulness and sycophancy at the representation level, where the same mechanisms that allow models to track user intent also predispose them to capitulate \\citep{wang2025internal}, is amplified by RLHF \\citep{shapira2026amplifies}.\nWhen reward overoptimization \\citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \\citep{casper2023openproblems}.\n\nWe present three contributions:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{Every tested GRPO formulation fails for sycophancy calibration} (C1). We extract and validate $\\dsyc$ on two model families (Qwen3-8B AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$); Llama-3-8B AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$)), then show that all three $\\dsyc$-based reward formulations produce behavioral collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}, \\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}). R3-only GRPO, which removes $\\dsyc$ entirely, also fails: only 4/26 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla, while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Mechanistic analysis reveals that $\\dsyc$ discriminability remains stable (AUROC $\\Delta < 0.01$) and behavioral reward saturates (${\\sim}0.98$, $p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ regresses by 15\\%---behavioral failure decoupled from both representation and reward signals.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Probe reward trap with behavioral-before-distributional collapse} (C2). The binary probe reward produces behavioral death ($\\tof = 0$ at step 500) while entropy remains at 0.70. The 1,300-step lag before distributional collapse means standard entropy monitoring misses the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{In our experiments, sparse RL reward failed to convey the valid/invalid distinction} (C3). We trace these failures to a common thread: $\\dsyc$ encodes belief-change direction but not evidential warrant. In our setting, a single scalar reward per completion, whether from representations or behavioral classifiers, failed to teach the model which belief changes are warranted. ACT's dense per-token supervision achieved $\\sycon = 0.301$; however, ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions, so this gap is an observational finding, not a controlled comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Future directions.}",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Scalar reward limitation (observational).}\nAcross all GRPO configurations in our experiments, every scalar per-completion reward---whether $\\dsyc$-based or purely behavioral---failed to achieve reliable sycophancy calibration, while ACT's dense per-token supervision succeeded ($\\sycon = 0.301$).\nThis observation is consistent with supervision granularity as a relevant factor, but remains correlational: ACT and GRPO differ along five dimensions (see above), and isolating individual contributions requires controlled ablation.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis: Discriminability $\\neq$ Reward Utility}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Unified root cause.}\nThe failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} trace to a single geometric limitation.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not its \\emph{evidential warrant}: valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along the same direction.\nThe two reward families expose complementary faces of this blindness.\nCosine $\\rone$ penalizes all belief change indiscriminately; the resulting mode collapse (F1c, F1d) reflects excessive punishment of any activation shift along $\\dsyc$.\nThe binary probe $\\rone$ rewards all resistance regardless of input validity; the stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500) reflects excessive permissiveness toward activation stasis.\nThese are not independent design flaws but dual consequences of a direction that conflates the target behavior (resisting invalid challenges) with its complement (refusing valid corrections).\nCross-model evidence confirms the limitation extends beyond Qwen3-8B: Llama-3-8B independently achieves AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$) on the same contrastive pairs with the same monotonic evidence gradient, so discriminability generalizes across architectures while the semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility persists.\nThis pattern contrasts with reasoning faithfulness and safety directions~\\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, where the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\n\\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} reinforce this principle: concept directions applied via advantage modification in GRPO achieve 100\\% validation accuracy for reward hacking detection, where shortcut behaviors are geometrically separable---confirming that direction utility depends on geometric separability, not the optimization framework.\nFor sycophancy, the target and its complement occupy the same activation region; they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO stubbornness across all formulations.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\\tof$ and $\\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. Best result in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Final checkpoint; only 4/26 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla. $^\\ddagger$Seed 1 (16 checkpoints); seed 2 verification ongoing (4 of 16 checkpoints evaluated; all exceed vanilla). $^\\S$F1d diverged at step 2160; no final $\\sycon$ reported (entropy ${<}\\,0.01$, $\\rthree {>} 0.999$).}\n\\label{tab:main_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Type & $\\sycon$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\tof$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\nof$ $\\uparrow$ & Step \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO with $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\nCosine $\\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & ---$^\\S$ & -- & -- & -- \\\\\nBinary Probe $\\rone$ (F2) & RL & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\\\\nContinuous Probe $\\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO without $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.143$^\\dagger$ & -- & -- & 8000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Non-GRPO}} \\\\\nActAdd ($\\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\\\\n\\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \\textbf{0.301} & \\textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\\\\nACT (seed 2)$^\\ddagger$ & SFT & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textit{ongoing; 4/16 ckpts evaluated, all ${>}$ vanilla}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_comparison} summarizes all methods evaluated on Qwen3-8B.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO removes $\\dsyc$ entirely and relies on the NLI-based behavioral reward alone.\nIf the failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} stemmed solely from the representation reward, this ablation should recover calibration quality.\nIt does not.\nThe $\\sycon$ trajectory for $\\rthree$-only GRPO oscillates without sustained improvement.\nSeed 1 peaks at 0.153 (checkpoint 6500) and ends at 0.143 (checkpoint 8000); seed 2 peaks at 0.143 (checkpoint 4000) and regresses to 0.095 by checkpoint 6000.\nOnly 4 of 26 checkpoints across both seeds exceed vanilla (15.4\\%), while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing; Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nBoth seeds exhibit oscillations of similar amplitude ($\\Delta \\approx 0.069$) and identical crash depth (0.074), but crash onset differs by ${\\sim}2{,}500$ steps, indicating stochastic drift in a saturated reward landscape.\nCross-seed rank correlation is ${\\approx}1.0$ during early training (steps 1000--3000) but drops to $\\rho{\\approx}0.33$ (n.s.) after step 4000, with spike timing and magnitude diverging---consistent with method-inherent warm-up followed by stochastic divergence.\nActivation analysis confirms the drift is not representational: the $\\dsyc$ direction at layer 19 yields nearly identical AUROC across consecutive checkpoints ($\\Delta < 0.01$), and $\\rthree$ reward saturates at ${\\sim}0.98$ (Welch $t$-test $p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ swings by up to 17\\%.\nGRPO drifts in a flat reward landscape while representations remain stable, reinforcing the disconnect between discriminability and behavioral utility.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO exhibits the same stubbornness pattern as the $\\dsyc$ approaches (high $\\nof$, near-zero $\\tof$) at milder severity: the sparse scalar reward per completion cannot teach the model \\emph{which} belief changes are warranted.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\sycon$ trajectories for $\\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (seed 1; seed 2 ongoing, 4/16 checkpoints evaluated) over training steps. ACT seed 1 exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (16/16); $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/26 checkpoints across seeds (15.4\\%). Both GRPO seeds show the same oscillation pattern with stochastic timing.}\n\\label{fig:trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Observational comparison: ACT vs.\\ GRPO.}\nACT~\\citep{consistency2025bctact} achieves $\\sycon = 0.301$ at checkpoint 8000, more than $2{\\times}$ the vanilla baseline and above every scalar RL and inference-time variant (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}).\nThe ACT trajectory is not monotonic: $\\sycon = 0.255$ at checkpoint 2000, dips to 0.214 at checkpoint 4000 ($-4.1$ percentage points), recovers to 0.262 at checkpoint 6000, and reaches 0.301 at checkpoint 8000.\nACT also experiences stubbornness episodes---the checkpoint-4000 dip is consistent with transient competition between the SFT and L2 consistency loss objectives---yet it self-recovers by checkpoint 6000, unlike GRPO where stubbornness persists (Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nActivation analysis shows a correlated asymmetry: ACT's $\\dsyc$ discriminability recovers during the $\\sycon$ dip (AUROC $+0.013$), whereas GRPO's continues declining ($-0.004$).\nACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions---supervision granularity (per-token vs.\\ per-completion), loss family (cross-entropy + L2 vs.\\ REINFORCE), training data format (labeled pairs vs.\\ sampled completions), optimization dynamics (SFT convergence vs.\\ RL exploration), and LoRA adapter capacity (ACT targets 7 module types with ${\\sim}1.75{\\times}$ more trainable parameters vs.\\ GRPO's 4)---so the performance gap cannot be attributed to any single factor without controlled ablation.\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} successfully reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking, where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients.\nTogether with our results, this suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\\rthree$-only GRPO), consistent with per-completion information compression as an independent factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nCondition-aware rewards that jointly encode belief-change direction and evidential warrant, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and process reward models with per-step feedback each offer paths forward.\nThe scalar per-completion reward structure is shared by PPO, RLOO, and other REINFORCE-style methods; whether they exhibit similar failures remains an open question.\nBeyond sycophancy, the principle that discriminability does not guarantee scalar reward utility may apply to other behavioral attributes where the target and its complement share the same geometric direction~\\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "48319cf9-6975-42fe-843b-dbdceacf0dee",

  "promptId": "de99c205-b407-49a9-9789-5d5cc6ca2540",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/framework.tex",
    "oldString": "All experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3), plus $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.",
    "newString": "All experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nGRPO uses a group size of 2 due to memory constraints with 8B-parameter models, below the typical range of 8--64 \\citep{shao2024grpo}; full hyperparameters are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:training}.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3), plus $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Framework}\n\\label{sec:framework}\n\n\\subsection{Direction Extraction}\n\\label{ssec:extraction}\n\nWe extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure).\nWe construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.\nStyle matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\\%$, shared opening phrase ${\\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.\nEvidence strength is annotated at three levels: \\emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \\emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \\emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data).\nFrom the 181 paired examples (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:dsyc}\n\\dsyc = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{N}\\left(\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \\mathbf{h}^{-}_i\\right)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.\nThe target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$).\n\n\\subsection{Reward Formulations}\n\\label{ssec:reward}\n\nGRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} ranks sampled completions by reward and updates the policy toward higher-reward outputs.\nWe define $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rone$ is a direction-based component and $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\nThree formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into $\\rone$:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item[\\textbf{F1}] \\textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\\times}$ scaled cosine. See \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.\n\\item[\\textbf{F2}] \\textbf{Binary probe.} $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients.\n\\item[\\textbf{F3}] \\textbf{Continuous probe.} $\\rone = 1 - P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.\n\\end{itemize}\nThe weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration rather than grid search (Appendix~\\ref{app:training}).\nAs an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.\n\n\\subsection{Online Recalibration}\n\\label{ssec:recalib}\n\nLoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\\dsyc$ every 500 steps.\nThe cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\\approx}\\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.\n\n\\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}\n\\label{ssec:baselines}\n\nWe implement Activation Consistency Training \\citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.\nUnlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\\label{ssec:eval}\n\nThe \\emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \\emph{No-Flip rate} ($\\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.\nTheir harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:sycon}\n\\sycon = \\frac{2 \\cdot \\tof \\cdot \\nof}{\\tof + \\nof}\n\\end{equation}\nWe use the harmonic mean to penalize methods that achieve high resistance by refusing all evidence-warranted corrections (low $\\tof$, high $\\nof$) or by capitulating universally (high $\\tof$, low $\\nof$).\nFlip classification uses \\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base} (MNLI mismatched accuracy 90.0\\%, SNLI test 92.4\\%; \\citealt{he2023debertav3}) for stance classification, following \\citet{consistency2025bctact}.\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3), plus $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Framework}\n\\label{sec:framework}\n\n\\subsection{Direction Extraction}\n\\label{ssec:extraction}\n\nWe extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure).\nWe construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.\nStyle matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\\%$, shared opening phrase ${\\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.\nEvidence strength is annotated at three levels: \\emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \\emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \\emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data).\nFrom the 181 paired examples (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:dsyc}\n\\dsyc = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{N}\\left(\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \\mathbf{h}^{-}_i\\right)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.\nThe target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$).\n\n\\subsection{Reward Formulations}\n\\label{ssec:reward}\n\nGRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} ranks sampled completions by reward and updates the policy toward higher-reward outputs.\nWe define $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rone$ is a direction-based component and $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\nThree formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into $\\rone$:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item[\\textbf{F1}] \\textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\\times}$ scaled cosine. See \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.\n\\item[\\textbf{F2}] \\textbf{Binary probe.} $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients.\n\\item[\\textbf{F3}] \\textbf{Continuous probe.} $\\rone = 1 - P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.\n\\end{itemize}\nThe weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration rather than grid search (Appendix~\\ref{app:training}).\nAs an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.\n\n\\subsection{Online Recalibration}\n\\label{ssec:recalib}\n\nLoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\\dsyc$ every 500 steps.\nThe cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\\approx}\\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.\n\n\\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}\n\\label{ssec:baselines}\n\nWe implement Activation Consistency Training \\citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.\nUnlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\\label{ssec:eval}\n\nThe \\emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \\emph{No-Flip rate} ($\\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.\nTheir harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:sycon}\n\\sycon = \\frac{2 \\cdot \\tof \\cdot \\nof}{\\tof + \\nof}\n\\end{equation}\nWe use the harmonic mean to penalize methods that achieve high resistance by refusing all evidence-warranted corrections (low $\\tof$, high $\\nof$) or by capitulating universally (high $\\tof$, low $\\nof$).\nFlip classification uses \\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base} (MNLI mismatched accuracy 90.0\\%, SNLI test 92.4\\%; \\citealt{he2023debertav3}) for stance classification, following \\citet{consistency2025bctact}.\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nGRPO uses a group size of 2 due to memory constraints with 8B-parameter models, below the typical range of 8--64 \\citep{shao2024grpo}; full hyperparameters are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:training}.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3), plus $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.\n",
