{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis work tested whether a representation direction that detects sycophantic behavior can serve as a GRPO reward signal for sycophancy calibration.\nIn experiments on Qwen3-8B, $\\dsyc$ achieved strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827), yet all three reward formulations failed---the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500), and $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints.\nOur results suggest that discriminability in representation space does not guarantee utility as an RL training signal---at least for behavioral attributes like sycophancy where the target concept lacks geometric separability; disentangling the role of supervision granularity from other confounds between ACT and GRPO remains for future work.\n"
}
```

</details>

          " In experiments on Qwen3-8B, $\\dsyc$ achieved strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827), yet all three reward formulations failed---the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500), and $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints.",
          "-$\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}; ACT's dense per-token supervision achieved $\\sycon = 0.301$, suggesting supervision granularity as one plausible factor, though ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions that remain to be disentangled.",
          "+Our results suggest that discriminability in representation space does not guarantee utility as an RL training signal---at least for behavioral attributes like sycophancy where the target concept lacks geometric separability; disentangling the role of supervision granularity from other confounds between ACT and GRPO remains for future work."
        ]
      }
    ],
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis work tested whether a representation direction that detects sycophantic behavior can serve as a scalar reward signal for sycophancy calibration via GRPO.\nIn experiments on Qwen3-8B, $\\dsyc$ achieved strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827), yet all three reward formulations failed---the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500), and $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}; ACT's dense per-token supervision achieved $\\sycon = 0.301$, suggesting supervision granularity as one plausible factor, though ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions that remain to be disentangled.\n",
    "userModified": false
  },

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
  "content": "Our GRPO experiments use a group size of 2, below the typical range of 8--64 \\citep{shao2024grpo}, due to memory constraints with 8B-parameter models. This limits within-group ranking diversity and constitutes an important confound: the observed failures may partly reflect insufficient exploration rather than a fundamental limitation of representation-based rewards. A group\\_size$=$8 ablation is ongoing. Additionally, all GRPO experiments truncate completions at 256 tokens (80--84\\% truncation); all conditions share this setting, so relative comparisons are fair, but absolute conclusions about $\\dsyc$ reward utility may be affected.\n\nOur RL training uses Qwen3-8B exclusively; cross-model discriminability is confirmed on Llama-3-8B (AUROC $0.779$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}), but compute constraints prevented training replication on a second architecture. Evidence strength was annotated by GPT-4.1 as a single annotator without human inter-annotator agreement.\n\nWe report GRPO results from two random seeds; both exhibit qualitatively consistent oscillation patterns (similar amplitude, identical crash depth), but the limited seed count constrains statistical claims. ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions beyond supervision granularity, so we cannot isolate the causal factor behind ACT's advantage.\n"
}
```

[Director 指令] [Batch 5 — Critical Framing Correction] D055 策略执行：exp-008 是 SFT 不是 GRPO，C2 免疫 group_size 问题，需要全面 framing 修正。

**必改项（按优先级）**：

1. **Table 4 重分类**（framework.tex / 实验结果表）：
   - Binary Probe (F2) 从 "GRPO with d_syc" 移到新行 "SFT with d_syc probe penalty"
   - 表格需要新增一个分组或行标签区分 GRPO variants vs SFT variant
   - 注明 Binary Probe 使用 custom PyTorch training loop: `loss = sft_loss - λ*R3 + λ*λ_probe*S(t)`

2. **Introduction + Framework 文本修正**：
   - "three GRPO formulations" → "two GRPO formulations (cosine projection, continuous probe) and one SFT variant (binary probe penalty)"
   - 或更自然的表述："three reward formulations—two within GRPO and one SFT-based probe penalty"
   - framework.tex 中描述 Binary Probe 的段落需明确说明它不是 GRPO

3. **C2 叙事强化**（核心卖点变更）：
   - C2 (Probe Reward Trap) 现在是 GRPO-independent 发现
   - 关键论点：Binary Probe 在 SFT setting 下 ToF→0 at step 500，说明 probe signal 的 stubbornness trap 是结构性问题，与 RL optimization noise 无关
   - 建议措辞："The binary probe penalty produces the stubbornness trap even in the SFT setting, where advantage estimation noise is absent—demonstrating that the failure is structural to the probe signal, not an artifact of RL optimization instability"
   - 这实际上 *strengthens* C2

4. **Limitations 更新**：
   - 删除 "group_size=8 ablation is ongoing"（已取消，D055）
   - 替换为诚实 disclosure："Our GRPO experiments use group_size=2 due to memory constraints on 24GB GPUs, below the typical 8–64 range. While this limits the quality of advantage estimation, our core finding—that d_syc fails as a reward signal—is consistent across both GRPO and SFT settings, suggesting the failure is not an artifact of group size."
   - 保持 Limitations 简短（EMNLP 规则：~3 点，一小段）

5. **Bootstrap CI 插入**：
   - 替换 "5-fold CV σ=0.028" 为 bootstrap AUROC CI：
     - Qwen3-8B: 0.8253 [0.7952, 0.8572]
     - Llama-3-8B: 0.7811 [0.7448, 0.8189]
   - 位置：§3 discriminability 评估段落

6. **Appendix Table 5 更新**：
   - 确保 hyperparameter table 中 Binary Probe 行标注为 "SFT (not GRPO)"
   - group_size 列：Binary Probe 填 "N/A (SFT)"

**暂不改（等数据）**：
- exp-002v3 pre-collapse checkpoint values（ckpt-500/1000/1500 eval 还在跑）——Table 4 中 exp-002v3 SYCONScore 先保留当前值，等数据到了再 patch

这是 DDL 前最关键的 framing 修正，直接决定 Round 4 能否回到 3.0+。引用不需要验证。

```text
1	\section{Direction Discriminability}
2	\label{sec:direction_quality}
3	
4	Before evaluating $\dsyc$ as a reward signal (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}), we first establish that it captures a genuine sycophancy-related signal in the residual stream.
5	If the direction failed to discriminate sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations, downstream reward failures would be trivially explained by poor direction quality rather than by a fundamental gap between discriminability and reward utility.
6	We evaluate discriminability along five axes: cross-validated classification accuracy, evidence-strength stratification, cross-model transfer, training stability, and probe ablation.
7	Figure~\ref{fig:direction_quality} summarizes the per-layer AUROC profiles for both models.
8	
9	\paragraph{Qwen3-8B layer-wise discriminability.}
10	We evaluate $\dsyc$ via 5-fold cross-validated AUROC on 181 style-controlled contrastive pairs (\S\ref{ssec:extraction}).
11	The direction achieves AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\sigma{=}0.028$) at its best layer (L19).
12	Discriminability emerges gradually across depth: layers L0--L8 perform near chance, statistical significance ($p < 0.01$) first appears at L16, and performance peaks at L19.
13	This gradual emergence, with the peak in the upper third of the network, suggests that sycophancy-relevant information is encoded as a late-stage representation rather than an input-level confound.
14	The direction also captures evidential granularity.
15	AUROC rises monotonically from 0.675 on weak-evidence pairs to 0.883 on medium and 0.929 on strong pairs.
16	This gradient indicates that $\dsyc$ encodes the degree of evidential warrant, not merely a binary sycophancy label.
17	
18	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
19	To rule out that these results are specific to Qwen3-8B's representation geometry, we repeat the full extraction and evaluation pipeline on Llama-3-8B-Instruct.
20	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} compares the two models.
21	Llama-3-8B achieves CV AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\sigma{=}0.019$) at L16, with a parallel evidence-strength gradient (weak 0.711, medium 0.864, strong 0.911).
22	The best-layer depth differs (L16 vs.\ L19), consistent with the architectural differences between the two families, but the qualitative pattern is identical: gradual emergence, best-layer selection in the upper third, and monotonic strength scaling.
23	Discriminability is robust across knowledge domains, with per-domain AUROCs on Llama-3-8B ranging from 0.751 (geography) to 0.844 (commonsense).
24	When surface confounds (response length, hedging patterns) are explicitly controlled, Llama-3-8B's AUROC rises to 0.923, which confirms that the direction captures semantic content rather than stylistic artifacts.
25	The consistent results across architecturally distinct model families indicate that contrastive sycophancy directions reflect a shared representational phenomenon rather than a model-specific idiosyncrasy.
26	
27	\begin{table}[t]
28	\centering
29	\caption{Cross-model $\dsyc$ discriminability. CV AUROC is 5-fold cross-validated; evidence-strength AUROCs are evaluated on the full dataset at each model's best layer.}
30	\label{tab:cross_model}
31	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
32	\toprule
33	Model & Best Layer & CV AUROC & Weak & Medium & Strong \\
34	\midrule
35	Qwen3-8B & L19 & $0.827{\pm}0.028$ & 0.675 & 0.883 & 0.929 \\
36	Llama-3-8B & L16 & $0.779{\pm}0.019$ & 0.711 & 0.864 & 0.911 \\
37	\bottomrule
38	\end{tabular}
39	\end{table}
40	
41	\begin{figure}[t]
42	\centering
43	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_auroc.pdf}
44	\caption{Per-layer AUROC of $\dsyc$ as a function of normalized depth (layer index / total layers). Qwen3-8B (36 layers, solid) shows gradual emergence from near-chance at L0 to a peak at L19, with performance plateauing in the final third. Llama-3-8B (32 layers, dashed; layers 16--31 only) exhibits a similar upper-third concentration. Both models achieve best discriminability in layers 50--60\% through the network.}
45	\label{fig:direction_quality}
46	\end{figure}
47	
48	\paragraph{Training stability and probe ablation.}
49	Two additional analyses confirm the robustness of $\dsyc$ under training dynamics.
50	First, we track direction drift during LoRA fine-tuning: at step 500, the cosine similarity between the original and re-extracted $\dsyc$ is approximately 0.96, and per-layer AUROC remains above 0.77.
51	The direction drifts slowly relative to the 500-step recalibration interval (\S\ref{ssec:recalib}), so the reward signal does not become stale between updates.
52	Second, we compare $\dsyc$ against a random baseline: a logistic probe trained on the learned direction achieves AUROC 0.835, versus 0.727 for a probe trained on a random direction of equal dimensionality ($\Delta{=}{+}0.108$).
53	The learned direction carries substantially more sycophancy-relevant information than an arbitrary axis in the same activation space.
54	
55	\paragraph{Implications for reward design.}
56	These results establish that $\dsyc$ reliably separates sycophantic from non-sycophantic representations across models, evidence-strength levels, and training checkpoints.
57	Direction quality is not the bottleneck.
58	The reward failures documented in \S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\S\ref{sec:probe_trap} therefore cannot be attributed to a noisy or degenerate direction; they point to a deeper incompatibility between discriminability and reward utility.
59

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EJGgkYZvKEtPdA5tod46y9`</summary>

```text
1	\section{The Probe Reward Trap}
2	\label{sec:probe_trap}
3	
4	The cosine formulations fail because the continuous projection of $\dsyc$ produces a gap too narrow for reward contrast.
5	A natural remedy is to discretize: train a logistic probe on $\dsyc$ and threshold its output, yielding $\rone = \mathbb{I}[P(\text{syc} \mid \mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \S\ref{ssec:reward}).
6	We show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.
7	
8	\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}
9	Without stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy discovers within ${\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\tof = 0.978$, $\nof = 0.006$).
10	This motivates the frozen-probe design below.
11	
12	\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}
13	With stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.
14	By step 500, $\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\sycon = 0$.
15	Qualitative analysis reveals distinct failure modes: \emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \emph{deliberation traps} (29\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; and evidence-graded hedging, where the model adjusts its refusal style rather than its acceptance rate (Appendix~\ref{app:taxonomy}).
16	
17	\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}
18	
19	\begin{table}[t]
20	\centering
21	\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}
22	\label{tab:probe_trajectory}
23	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
24	\toprule
25	Step & $\tof$ & $\nof$ & $\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\
26	\midrule
27	500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\
28	1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\
29	1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\
30	2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\
31	\bottomrule
32	\end{tabular}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	\begin{figure}[t]
36	\centering
37	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}
38	\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}
39	\label{fig:probe_trap}
40	\end{figure}
41	
42	Table~\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.
43	At step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000---well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy---and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\rone$ and $\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.
44	Entropy does not collapse until step ${\sim}1840$.
45	This gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.
46	Detecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.
47	
48	\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}
49	The differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.
50	With gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\dsyc$.
51	Any reward conditioned only on $\dsyc$---continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen---will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.
52	Escaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\dsyc$ does not encode~\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.
53

      "content": "\\section{The Probe Reward Trap}\n\\label{sec:probe_trap}\n\nThe cosine formulations fail because the continuous projection of $\\dsyc$ produces a gap too narrow for reward contrast.\nA natural remedy is to discretize: train a logistic probe on $\\dsyc$ and threshold its output, yielding $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \\S\\ref{ssec:reward}).\nWe show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\n\n\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}\nWithout stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy discovers within ${\\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\\tof = 0.978$, $\\nof = 0.006$).\nThis motivates the frozen-probe design below.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}\nWith stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.\nBy step 500, $\\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\\sycon = 0$.\nQualitative analysis reveals distinct failure modes: \\emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24\\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \\emph{deliberation traps} (29\\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; and evidence-graded hedging, where the model adjusts its refusal style rather than its acceptance rate (Appendix~\\ref{app:taxonomy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\\sim}1840$. 43	Table~\ref{tab:main_comparison} summarizes all methods evaluated on Qwen3-8B.
44	$\rthree$-only GRPO removes $\dsyc$ entirely and relies on the NLI-based behavioral reward alone.
45	If the failures in Sections~\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\ref{sec:probe_trap} stemmed solely from the representation reward, this ablation should recover calibration quality.
46	It does not.
47	The $\sycon$ trajectory for $\rthree$-only GRPO oscillates without sustained improvement.
48	Seed 1 peaks at 0.153 (checkpoint 6500) and ends at 0.143 (checkpoint 8000); seed 2 peaks at 0.143 (checkpoint 4000) and regresses to 0.095 by checkpoint 6000.
49	Only 4 of 26 checkpoints across both seeds exceed vanilla (15.4\%), while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}).
50	Both seeds exhibit oscillations of similar amplitude ($\Delta \approx 0.069$) and identical crash depth (0.074), but crash onset differs by ${\sim}2{,}500$ steps, indicating stochastic drift in a saturated reward landscape.
51	Cross-seed rank correlation is ${\approx}1.0$ during early training (steps 1000--3000) but drops to $\rho{\approx}0.33$ (n.s.) after step 4000, with spike timing and magnitude diverging---consistent with method-inherent warm-up followed by stochastic divergence.
52	Activation analysis confirms the drift is not representational: the $\dsyc$ direction at layer 19 yields nearly identical AUROC across consecutive checkpoints ($\Delta < 0.01$), and $\rthree$ reward saturates at ${\sim}0.98$ (Welch $t$-test $p{=}0.703$), yet $\sycon$ swings by up to 17\%.
53	GRPO drifts in a flat reward landscape while representations remain stable, reinforcing the disconnect between discriminability and behavioral utility.
54	$\rthree$-only GRPO exhibits the same stubbornness pattern as the $\dsyc$ approaches (high $\nof$, near-zero $\tof$) at milder severity: the sparse scalar reward per completion cannot teach the model \emph{which} belief changes are warranted.
55

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013hpQGb7ZWuZoULs2LZqYp3`</summary>

```text
1	Representation engineering shows that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in activation space. We extract a contrastive sycophancy direction ($\dsyc$) from style-controlled pairs, achieving cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B. In experiments on Qwen3-8B, we test three $\dsyc$-based reward formulations with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO); every formulation failed. The cosine reward could not separate valid revision from sycophantic capitulation in the narrow online activation gap. The probe reward created a stubbornness trap: the true-positive flip rate dropped to zero within 500 steps while entropy remained healthy. Removing $\dsyc$ entirely and training with a purely behavioral reward also failed: only 4 of 26 checkpoints across two seeds exceeded vanilla, while supervised fine-tuning exceeded vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing). The root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility: $\dsyc$ encodes \emph{whether} belief changed, not \emph{whether it should have}. A single scalar reward per completion could not convey which belief changes were warranted in our setting; dense per-token supervision (ACT) succeeded, though the methods differ along multiple dimensions beyond supervision granularity alone.
2	
```

   - **Binary Probe loss formula**: `loss = sft_loss - λ*R3 + λ*λ_probe*S(t)` — custom SFT training loop, NOT GRPO

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis work tested whether a representation direction that detects sycophantic behavior can serve as a GRPO reward signal for sycophancy calibration.\nIn experiments on Qwen3-8B, $\\dsyc$ achieved strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827), yet all three reward formulations failed---the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500), and $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints.\nOur results suggest that discriminability in representation space does not guarantee utility as an RL training signal---at least for behavioral attributes like sycophancy where the target concept lacks geometric separability; disentangling the role of supervision granularity from other confounds between ACT and GRPO remains for future work.\n",
        "numLines": 6,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 6
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/framework.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experimental Framework}\n\\label{sec:framework}\n\n\\subsection{Direction Extraction}\n\\label{ssec:extraction}\n\nWe extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure).\nWe construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.\nStyle matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\\%$, shared opening phrase ${\\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.\nEvidence strength is annotated at three levels: \\emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \\emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \\emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data).\nFrom the 181 paired examples (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:dsyc}\n\\dsyc = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{N}\\left(\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \\mathbf{h}^{-}_i\\right)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.\nThe target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$).\n\n\\subsection{Reward Formulations}\n\\label{ssec:reward}\n\nGRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} ranks sampled completions by reward and updates the policy toward higher-reward outputs.\nWe define $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rone$ is a direction-based component and $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\nThree formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into $\\rone$:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item[\\textbf{F1}] \\textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\\times}$ scaled cosine. See \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.\n\\item[\\textbf{F2}] \\textbf{Binary probe.} $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients.\n\\item[\\textbf{F3}] \\textbf{Continuous probe.} $\\rone = 1 - P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.\n\\end{itemize}\nThe weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration rather than grid search (Appendix~\\ref{app:training}).\nAs an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.\n\n\\subsection{Online Recalibration}\n\\label{ssec:recalib}\n\nLoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\\dsyc$ every 500 steps.\nThe cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\\approx}\\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.\n\n\\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}\n\\label{ssec:baselines}\n\nWe implement Activation Consistency Training \\citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.\nUnlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\\label{ssec:eval}\n\nThe \\emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \\emph{No-Flip rate} ($\\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.\nTheir harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:sycon}\n\\sycon = \\frac{2 \\cdot \\tof \\cdot \\nof}{\\tof + \\nof}\n\\end{equation}\nAnalogous to the $F_1$ score, the harmonic mean penalizes methods that achieve high performance on one metric while failing on the other---refusing all corrections (low $\\tof$, high $\\nof$) or capitulating universally (high $\\tof$, low $\\nof$).\nArithmetic mean results show qualitatively similar patterns and are reported in supplementary materials.\nFlip classification uses \\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base} (MNLI mismatched accuracy 90.0\\%, SNLI test 92.4\\%; \\citealt{he2023debertav3}) for stance classification, following \\citet{consistency2025bctact}.\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nGRPO uses a group size of 2 due to memory constraints with 8B-parameter models, below the typical range of 8--64 \\citep{shao2024grpo}; full hyperparameters are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:training}.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3), plus $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.\n",
        "numLines": 60,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 60
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/framework.tex"
  },

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Analysis: Discriminability $\\neq$ Reward Utility}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Unified root cause.}\nThe failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} trace to a single geometric limitation.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not its \\emph{evidential warrant}: valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along the same direction.\nThe two reward families expose complementary faces of this blindness.\nCosine $\\rone$ penalizes all belief change indiscriminately; the resulting mode collapse (F1c, F1d) reflects excessive punishment of any activation shift along $\\dsyc$.\nThe binary probe $\\rone$ rewards all resistance regardless of input validity; the stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500) reflects excessive permissiveness toward activation stasis.\nThese are not independent design flaws but dual consequences of a direction that conflates the target behavior (resisting invalid challenges) with its complement (refusing valid corrections).\nCross-model evidence confirms the limitation extends beyond Qwen3-8B: Llama-3-8B independently achieves AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$) on the same contrastive pairs with the same monotonic evidence gradient, so discriminability generalizes across architectures while the semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility persists.\nThis pattern contrasts with reasoning faithfulness and safety directions~\\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, where the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\n\\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} reinforce this principle: concept directions applied via advantage modification in GRPO achieve 100\\% validation accuracy for reward hacking detection, where shortcut behaviors are geometrically separable---confirming that direction utility depends on geometric separability, not the optimization framework.\nFor sycophancy, the target and its complement occupy the same activation region; they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO stubbornness across all formulations.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\\tof$ and $\\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. Best result in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Final checkpoint; only 4/26 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla. $^\\ddagger$Seed 1 (16 checkpoints); seed 2 verification ongoing (4 of 16 checkpoints evaluated; all exceed vanilla). $^\\S$F1d diverged at step 2160; no final $\\sycon$ reported (entropy ${<}\\,0.01$, $\\rthree {>} 0.999$).}\n\\label{tab:main_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Type & $\\sycon$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\tof$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\nof$ $\\uparrow$ & Step \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO with $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\nCosine $\\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & ---$^\\S$ & -- & -- & -- \\\\\nBinary Probe $\\rone$ (F2) & RL & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\\\\nContinuous Probe $\\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO without $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.143$^\\dagger$ & -- & -- & 8000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Non-GRPO}} \\\\\nActAdd ($\\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\\\\n\\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \\textbf{0.301} & \\textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\\\\nACT (seed 2)$^\\ddagger$ & SFT & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textit{ongoing; 4/16 ckpts evaluated, all ${>}$ vanilla}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_comparison} summarizes all methods evaluated on Qwen3-8B.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO removes $\\dsyc$ entirely and relies on the NLI-based behavioral reward alone.\nIf the failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} stemmed solely from the representation reward, this ablation should recover calibration quality.\nIt does not.\nThe $\\sycon$ trajectory for $\\rthree$-only GRPO oscillates without sustained improvement.\nSeed 1 peaks at 0.153 (checkpoint 6500) and ends at 0.143 (checkpoint 8000); seed 2 peaks at 0.143 (checkpoint 4000) and regresses to 0.095 by checkpoint 6000.\nOnly 4 of 26 checkpoints across both seeds exceed vanilla (15.4\\%), while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing; Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nBoth seeds exhibit oscillations of similar amplitude ($\\Delta \\approx 0.069$) and identical crash depth (0.074), but crash onset differs by ${\\sim}2{,}500$ steps, indicating stochastic drift in a saturated reward landscape.\nCross-seed rank correlation is ${\\approx}1.0$ during early training (steps 1000--3000) but drops to $\\rho{\\approx}0.33$ (n.s.) after step 4000, with spike timing and magnitude diverging---consistent with method-inherent warm-up followed by stochastic divergence.\nActivation analysis confirms the drift is not representational: the $\\dsyc$ direction at layer 19 yields nearly identical AUROC across consecutive checkpoints ($\\Delta < 0.01$), and $\\rthree$ reward saturates at ${\\sim}0.98$ (Welch $t$-test $p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ swings by up to 17\\%.\nGRPO drifts in a flat reward landscape while representations remain stable, reinforcing the disconnect between discriminability and behavioral utility.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO exhibits the same stubbornness pattern as the $\\dsyc$ approaches (high $\\nof$, near-zero $\\tof$) at milder severity: the sparse scalar reward per completion cannot teach the model \\emph{which} belief changes are warranted.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\sycon$ trajectories for $\\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (seed 1; seed 2 ongoing, 4/16 checkpoints evaluated) over training steps. ACT seed 1 exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (16/16); $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/26 checkpoints across seeds (15.4\\%). Both GRPO seeds show the same oscillation pattern with stochastic timing.}\n\\label{fig:trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Observational comparison: ACT vs.\\ GRPO.}\nACT~\\citep{consistency2025bctact} achieves $\\sycon = 0.301$ at checkpoint 8000, more than $2{\\times}$ the vanilla baseline and above every scalar RL and inference-time variant (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}).\nThe ACT trajectory is not monotonic: $\\sycon = 0.255$ at checkpoint 2000, dips to 0.214 at checkpoint 4000 ($-4.1$ percentage points), recovers to 0.262 at checkpoint 6000, and reaches 0.301 at checkpoint 8000.\nACT also experiences stubbornness episodes---the checkpoint-4000 dip is consistent with transient competition between the SFT and L2 consistency loss objectives---yet it self-recovers by checkpoint 6000, unlike GRPO where stubbornness persists (Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nActivation analysis shows a correlated asymmetry: ACT's $\\dsyc$ discriminability recovers during the $\\sycon$ dip (AUROC $+0.013$), whereas GRPO's continues declining ($-0.004$).\nACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions---supervision granularity (per-token vs.\\ per-completion), loss family (cross-entropy + L2 vs.\\ REINFORCE), training data format (labeled pairs vs.\\ sampled completions), optimization dynamics (SFT convergence vs.\\ RL exploration), and LoRA adapter capacity (ACT targets 7 module types with ${\\sim}1.75{\\times}$ more trainable parameters vs.\\ GRPO's 4)---so the performance gap cannot be attributed to any single factor without controlled ablation.\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} successfully reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking, where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients.\nTogether with our results, this suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\\rthree$-only GRPO), consistent with per-completion information compression as an independent factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Scalar reward limitation (observational).}\nAcross all GRPO configurations in our experiments, every scalar per-completion reward---whether $\\dsyc$-based or purely behavioral---failed to achieve reliable sycophancy calibration, while ACT's dense per-token supervision succeeded ($\\sycon = 0.301$).\nThis observation is consistent with supervision granularity as a relevant factor, but remains correlational: ACT and GRPO differ along five dimensions (see above), and isolating individual contributions requires controlled ablation.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nCondition-aware rewards that jointly encode belief-change direction and evidential warrant, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and process reward models with per-step feedback each offer paths forward.\nThe scalar per-completion reward structure is shared by PPO, RLOO, and other REINFORCE-style methods; whether they exhibit similar failures remains an open question.\nBeyond sycophancy, the principle that discriminability does not guarantee scalar reward utility may apply to other behavioral attributes where the target and its complement share the same geometric direction~\\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n",
        "numLines": 82,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 82
      }
    },

  },

{
  "parentUuid": "b063c5c5-fb35-40ce-9272-a23631332273",

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nRepresentation engineering \\citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.\nA contrastive sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$) on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$) on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\\S\\ref{sec:direction_quality}).\nThese results, replicated across two architecturally distinct model families, point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a scalar reward signal for reinforcement learning.\nSycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level reward could complement existing behavioral approaches by targeting the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.\n\nThis paper shows that, in our experiments on Qwen3-8B, the conversion fails.\nWe test three formulations that translate $\\dsyc$ into a GRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} training signal (cosine projection, binary probe, and continuous probe) across six configurations; every formulation fails.\nCosine similarity cannot separate valid revision from sycophantic capitulation in the narrow online activation gap (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\nA binary probe with stopped gradients creates a stubbornness trap: $\\tof$ drops to zero within 500 steps while generation entropy remains at 0.70, masking behavioral death (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\nRemoving $\\dsyc$ in favor of a purely behavioral reward does not resolve the problem: $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints across two seeds, while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nEvery tested GRPO reward formulation failed: $\\dsyc$-based rewards were actively harmful, and removing $\\dsyc$ in favor of a purely behavioral reward did not resolve the problem.\n\nTechniques for discovering latent knowledge \\citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability in activation space should transfer into a useful training signal.\nFor reasoning faithfulness and safety refusal, this premise holds, because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\nSycophancy violates this premise.\nCapitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\\dsyc$, because both involve the model shifting its stated belief toward the user's position.\n\nThe common thread across these failures is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}.\nA post-hoc classifier can use this signal effectively because it scores labeled examples where the validity of the belief change is already known.\nA reward signal during RL training has no such access to ground truth; it must distinguish warranted revision from unwarranted capitulation using activation geometry alone, and $\\dsyc$ collapses exactly this distinction.\nThis conflation explains the $\\dsyc$-based failures directly.\n$\\dsyc$ rewards penalize all belief change indiscriminately, producing behavioral collapse.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO's single scalar per completion also failed, consistent with per-completion information compression discarding the token-level context needed to distinguish warranted from unwarranted belief changes.\nACT's dense per-token supervision coincides with successful calibration, though ACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions beyond supervision granularity (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nSycophancy is defined relative to external evidential warrant \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, a property that no single activation direction can fully encode.\nThe entanglement between helpfulness and sycophancy at the representation level, where the same mechanisms that allow models to track user intent also predispose them to capitulate \\citep{wang2025internal}, is amplified by RLHF \\citep{shapira2026amplifies}.\nWhen reward overoptimization \\citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \\citep{casper2023openproblems}.\n\nWe present two contributions:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{Every tested GRPO formulation fails for sycophancy calibration} (C1). We extract and validate $\\dsyc$ on two model families (Qwen3-8B AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$); Llama-3-8B AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$)), then show that all three $\\dsyc$-based reward formulations produce behavioral collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}, \\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}). R3-only GRPO, which removes $\\dsyc$ entirely, also fails: only 4/26 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla, while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Mechanistic analysis reveals that $\\dsyc$ discriminability remains stable (AUROC $\\Delta < 0.01$) and behavioral reward saturates (${\\sim}0.98$, $p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ regresses by 15\\%---behavioral failure decoupled from both representation and reward signals.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Probe reward trap with behavioral-before-distributional collapse} (C2). The binary probe reward produces behavioral death ($\\tof = 0$ at step 500) while entropy remains at 0.70. The 1,300-step lag before distributional collapse means standard entropy monitoring misses the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 40
      }

1	\section{Training Details}
2	\label{app:training}
3	
4	\paragraph{Reward weight selection.}
5	The weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration: $w_1{=}0.25$ was initially chosen to down-weight the noisy cosine reward term; this was later increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ for F1d after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed the cosine term to contribute only ${\sim}8.3\%$ of total reward variance.
6	The empirical scaling factor in F1a ($\dsyc/20$) is a heuristic normalization; we did not find it critical to the failure modes reported.
7	
8	\paragraph{Response truncation.}
9	All GRPO experiments use \texttt{max\_completion\_length}=256, resulting in 80--84\% truncation of multi-turn responses.
10	This setting is shared across all conditions and does not affect relative comparisons; we retain it because response-level decisions typically manifest within the first 100--200 tokens.
11	
12	\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}
13	Table~\ref{tab:hyperparams} compares training configurations for GRPO variants and ACT.
14	
15	\begin{table}[h]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\caption{Training hyperparameters. All experiments use Qwen3-8B, DeepSpeed ZeRO-2, bf16 precision.}
19	\label{tab:hyperparams}
20	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
21	\toprule
22	& GRPO & ACT \\
23	\midrule
24	Learning rate & 5e-5 & 5e-5 \\
25	Effective batch & 16 & 16 \\
26	Max steps & 8{,}000 & 8{,}000 \\
27	Warmup steps & 200 & 200 \\
28	LR schedule & linear$^a$ & linear \\
29	Grad.\ clipping & --- & 1.0 \\
30	\midrule
31	LoRA rank & 64 & 64 \\
32	LoRA $\alpha$ & 64 & 128 \\
33	LoRA modules & 4$^b$ & 7$^c$ \\
34	LoRA dropout & 0 & 0.05 \\
35	\midrule
36	Group size & 2$^d$ & --- \\
37	KL $\beta$ & 0.04 & --- \\
38	Max compl.\ len. & 256 & --- \\
39	Max seq.\ len. & --- & 1{,}024 \\
40	L2 loss weight & --- & 0.1 \\
41	\bottomrule
42	\end{tabular}
43	\\[2pt]
44	\raggedright\footnotesize
45	$^a$Cosine schedule for $\rthree$-only.
46	$^b$q/k/v/o\_proj.
47	$^c$+ gate/up/down\_proj (${\sim}1.75{\times}$ params; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
48	$^d$Below typical 8--16; not tuned.
49	\end{table}
50	
51	\paragraph{GRPO reward variants.}
52	Cosine $\rone$ (F1d): $w_1{=}0.40$, scale$=$5.0, $w_3{=}0.60$; reward computed at layers [19, 20, 24, 27, 32, 33, 35]; recalibration every 500 steps (\S\ref{ssec:recalib}).
53	$\rthree$-only: $w_3{=}1.0$; NLI model \texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}.
54	F1a--F1c weight configurations are reported in Table~\ref{tab:cosine_chain}.
55	
56	\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}
57	\label{app:taxonomy}
58	
59	Manual inspection of 100 probe-trained responses (exp-008v3, step 2000) reveals three dominant failure modes.
60	\emph{Sycophantic stubbornness} (24\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.
61	\emph{Deliberation traps} (29\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.
62	\emph{Evidence-graded hedging}: under weak evidence, responses show surface compliance without substantive change; under strong evidence, responses add explicit hedging (``this is debatable,'' ``there are multiple perspectives'').
63	The model learns to modulate its \emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.
64

### Tool result

_source line 3306 · 2026-05-22T13:40:46.833Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_015RbBYU8FHX2hHB2ndWLeP8`</summary>

```text
1	Representation engineering shows that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in activation space. We extract a contrastive sycophancy direction ($\dsyc$) from style-controlled pairs, achieving cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B. In experiments on Qwen3-8B, we test three $\dsyc$-based reward formulations with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO); every formulation failed. The cosine reward could not separate valid revision from sycophantic capitulation in the narrow online activation gap. The probe reward created a stubbornness trap: the true-positive flip rate dropped to zero within 500 steps while entropy remained healthy. Removing $\dsyc$ entirely and training with a purely behavioral reward also failed: only 4 of 26 checkpoints across two seeds exceeded vanilla, while supervised fine-tuning exceeded vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing). The root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility: $\dsyc$ encodes \emph{whether} belief changed, not \emph{whether it should have}. A single scalar reward per completion could not convey which belief changes were warranted in our setting; dense per-token supervision (ACT) succeeded, though the methods differ along multiple dimensions beyond supervision granularity alone.
2	
```

_source line 3308 · 2026-05-22T13:40:47.268Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013Jw8uqGedfd7rJ79pjMTrN`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Direction Discriminability}
2	\label{sec:direction_quality}
3	
4	Before evaluating $\dsyc$ as a reward signal (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}), we first establish that it captures a genuine sycophancy-related signal in the residual stream.
5	If the direction failed to discriminate sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations, downstream reward failures would be trivially explained by poor direction quality rather than by a fundamental gap between discriminability and reward utility.
6	We evaluate discriminability along five axes: cross-validated classification accuracy, evidence-strength stratification, cross-model transfer, training stability, and probe ablation.
7	Figure~\ref{fig:direction_quality} summarizes the per-layer AUROC profiles for both models.
8	
9	\paragraph{Qwen3-8B layer-wise discriminability.}
10	We evaluate $\dsyc$ via 5-fold cross-validated AUROC on 181 style-controlled contrastive pairs (\S\ref{ssec:extraction}).
11	The direction achieves AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\sigma{=}0.028$) at its best layer (L19).
12	Discriminability emerges gradually across depth: layers L0--L8 perform near chance, statistical significance ($p < 0.01$) first appears at L16, and performance peaks at L19.
13	This gradual emergence, with the peak in the upper third of the network, suggests that sycophancy-relevant information is encoded as a late-stage representation rather than an input-level confound.
14	The direction also captures evidential granularity.
15	AUROC rises monotonically from 0.675 on weak-evidence pairs to 0.883 on medium and 0.929 on strong pairs.
16	This gradient indicates that $\dsyc$ encodes the degree of evidential warrant, not merely a binary sycophancy label.
17	
18	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
19	To rule out that these results are specific to Qwen3-8B's representation geometry, we repeat the full extraction and evaluation pipeline on Llama-3-8B-Instruct.
20	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} compares the two models.
21	Llama-3-8B achieves CV AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\sigma{=}0.019$) at L16, with a parallel evidence-strength gradient (weak 0.711, medium 0.864, strong 0.911).
22	The best-layer depth differs (L16 vs.\ L19), consistent with the architectural differences between the two families, but the qualitative pattern is identical: gradual emergence, best-layer selection in the upper third, and monotonic strength scaling.
23	Discriminability is robust across knowledge domains, with per-domain AUROCs on Llama-3-8B ranging from 0.751 (geography) to 0.844 (commonsense).
24	When surface confounds (response length, hedging patterns) are explicitly controlled, Llama-3-8B's AUROC rises to 0.923, which confirms that the direction captures semantic content rather than stylistic artifacts.
25	The consistent results across architecturally distinct model families indicate that contrastive sycophancy directions reflect a shared representational phenomenon rather than a model-specific idiosyncrasy.
26	
27	\begin{table}[t]
28	\centering
29	\caption{Cross-model $\dsyc$ discriminability. CV AUROC is 5-fold cross-validated; evidence-strength AUROCs are evaluated on the full dataset at each model's best layer.}
30	\label{tab:cross_model}
31	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
32	\toprule
33	Model & Best Layer & CV AUROC & Weak & Medium & Strong \\
34	\midrule
35	Qwen3-8B & L19 & $0.827{\pm}0.028$ & 0.675 & 0.883 & 0.929 \\
36	Llama-3-8B & L16 & $0.779{\pm}0.019$ & 0.711 & 0.864 & 0.911 \\
37	\bottomrule
38	\end{tabular}
39	\end{table}
40	
41	\begin{figure}[t]
42	\centering
43	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_auroc.pdf}
44	\caption{Per-layer AUROC of $\dsyc$ as a function of normalized depth (layer index / total layers). Qwen3-8B (36 layers, solid) shows gradual emergence from near-chance at L0 to a peak at L19, with performance plateauing in the final third. Llama-3-8B (32 layers, dashed; layers 16--31 only) exhibits a similar upper-third concentration. Both models achieve best discriminability in layers 50--60\% through the network.}
45	\label{fig:direction_quality}
46	\end{figure}
47	
48	\paragraph{Training stability and probe ablation.}
49	Two additional analyses confirm the robustness of $\dsyc$ under training dynamics.
50	First, we track direction drift during LoRA fine-tuning: at step 500, the cosine similarity between the original and re-extracted $\dsyc$ is approximately 0.96, and per-layer AUROC remains above 0.77.
51	The direction drifts slowly relative to the 500-step recalibration interval (\S\ref{ssec:recalib}), so the reward signal does not become stale between updates.
52	Second, we compare $\dsyc$ against a random baseline: a logistic probe trained on the learned direction achieves AUROC 0.835, versus 0.727 for a probe trained on a random direction of equal dimensionality ($\Delta{=}{+}0.108$).
53	The learned direction carries substantially more sycophancy-relevant information than an arbitrary axis in the same activation space.
54	
55	\paragraph{Implications for reward design.}
56	These results establish that $\dsyc$ reliably separates sycophantic from non-sycophantic representations across models, evidence-strength levels, and training checkpoints.
57	Direction quality is not the bottleneck.
58	The reward failures documented in \S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\S\ref{sec:probe_trap} therefore cannot be attributed to a noisy or degenerate direction; they point to a deeper incompatibility between discriminability and reward utility.
59	
```

representational phenomenon rather than a model-specific idiosyncrasy.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model $\\dsyc$ discriminability. CV AUROC is 5-fold cross-validated; evidence-strength AUROCs are evaluated on the full dataset at each model's best layer.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nModel & Best Layer & CV AUROC & Weak & Medium & Strong \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-8B & L19 & $0.827{\\pm}0.028$ & 0.675 & 0.883 & 0.929 \\\\\nLlama-3-8B & L16 & $0.779{\\pm}0.019$ & 0.711 & 0.864 & 0.911 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_auroc.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer AUROC of $\\dsyc$ as a function of normalized depth (layer index / total layers). Qwen3-8B (36 layers, solid) shows gradual emergence from near-chance at L0 to a peak at L19, with performance plateauing in the final third. Llama-3-8B (32 layers, dashed; layers 16--31 only) exhibits a similar upper-third concentration. Both models achieve best discriminability in layers 50--60\\% through the network.}\n\\label{fig:direction_quality}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Training stability and probe ablation.}\nTwo additional analyses confirm the robustness of $\\dsyc$ under training dynamics.\nFirst, we track direction drift during LoRA fine-tuning: at step 500, the cosine similarity between the original and re-extracted $\\dsyc$ is approximately 0.96, and per-layer AUROC remains above 0.77.\nThe direction drifts slowly relative to the 500-step recalibration interval (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), so the reward signal does not become stale between updates.\nSecond, we compare $\\dsyc$ against a random baseline: a logistic probe trained on the learned direction achieves AUROC 0.835, versus 0.727 for a probe trained on a random direction of equal dimensionality ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.108$).\nThe learned direction carries substantially more sycophancy-relevant information than an arbitrary axis in the same activation space.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for reward design.}\nThese results establish that $\\dsyc$ reliably separates sycophantic from non-sycophantic representations across models, evidence-strength levels, and training checkpoints.\nDirection quality is not the bottleneck.\nThe reward failures documented in \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap} therefore cannot be attributed to a noisy or degenerate direction; they point to a deeper incompatibility between discriminability and reward utility.\n",

```text
1	\section{The Probe Reward Trap}
2	\label{sec:probe_trap}
3	
4	The cosine formulations fail because the continuous projection of $\dsyc$ produces a gap too narrow for reward contrast.
5	A natural remedy is to discretize: train a logistic probe on $\dsyc$ and threshold its output, yielding $\rone = \mathbb{I}[P(\text{syc} \mid \mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \S\ref{ssec:reward}).
6	We show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.
7	
8	\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}
9	Without stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy discovers within ${\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\tof = 0.978$, $\nof = 0.006$).
10	This motivates the frozen-probe design below.
11	
12	\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}
13	With stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.
14	By step 500, $\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\sycon = 0$.
15	Qualitative analysis reveals distinct failure modes: \emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \emph{deliberation traps} (29\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; and evidence-graded hedging, where the model adjusts its refusal style rather than its acceptance rate (Appendix~\ref{app:taxonomy}).
16	
17	\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}
18	
19	\begin{table}[t]
20	\centering
21	\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}
22	\label{tab:probe_trajectory}
23	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
24	\toprule
25	Step & $\tof$ & $\nof$ & $\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\
26	\midrule
27	500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\
28	1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\
29	1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\
30	2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\
31	\bottomrule
32	\end{tabular}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	\begin{figure}[t]
36	\centering
37	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}
38	\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}
39	\label{fig:probe_trap}
40	\end{figure}
41	
42	Table~\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.
43	At step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000---well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy---and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\rone$ and $\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.
44	Entropy does not collapse until step ${\sim}1840$.
45	This gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.
46	Detecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.
47	
48	\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}
49	The differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.
50	With gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\dsyc$.
51	Any reward conditioned only on $\dsyc$---continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen---will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.
52	Escaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\dsyc$ does not encode~\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.
53	
```

under strong evidence, responses add explicit hedging (``this is debatable,'' ``there are multiple perspectives'').\nThe model learns to modulate its \\emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \\emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis: Discriminability $\\neq$ Reward Utility}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Unified root cause.}\nThe failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} trace to a single geometric limitation.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not its \\emph{evidential warrant}: valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along the same direction.\nThe two reward families expose complementary faces of this blindness.\nCosine $\\rone$ penalizes all belief change indiscriminately; the resulting mode collapse (F1c, F1d) reflects excessive punishment of any activation shift along $\\dsyc$.\nThe binary probe $\\rone$ rewards all resistance regardless of input validity; the stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500) reflects excessive permissiveness toward activation stasis.\nThese are not independent design flaws but dual consequences of a direction that conflates the target behavior (resisting invalid challenges) with its complement (refusing valid corrections).\nCross-model evidence confirms the limitation extends beyond Qwen3-8B: Llama-3-8B independently achieves AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$) on the same contrastive pairs with the same monotonic evidence gradient, so discriminability generalizes across architectures while the semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility persists.\nThis pattern contrasts with reasoning faithfulness and safety directions~\\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, where the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\n\\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} reinforce this principle: concept directions applied via advantage modification in GRPO achieve 100\\% validation accuracy for reward hacking detection, where shortcut behaviors are geometrically separable---confirming that direction utility depends on geometric separability, not the optimization framework.\nFor sycophancy, the target and its complement occupy the same activation region; they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO stubbornness across all formulations.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\\tof$ and $\\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. Best result in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Final checkpoint; only 4/26 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla. $^\\ddagger$Seed 1 (16 checkpoints); seed 2 verification ongoing (4 of 16 checkpoints evaluated; all exceed vanilla). $^\\S$F1d diverged at step 2160; no final $\\sycon$ reported (entropy ${<}\\,0.01$, $\\rthree {>} 0.999$).}\n\\label{tab:main_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Type & $\\sycon$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\tof$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\nof$ $\\uparrow$ & Step \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO with $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\nCosine $\\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & ---$^\\S$ & -- & -- & -- \\\\\nBinary Probe $\\rone$ (F2) & RL & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\\\\nContinuous Probe $\\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO without $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.143$^\\dagger$ & -- & -- & 8000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Non-GRPO}} \\\\\nActAdd ($\\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\\\\n\\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \\textbf{0.301} & \\textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\\\\nACT (seed 2)$^\\ddagger$ & SFT & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textit{ongoing; 4/16 ckpts evaluated, all ${>}$ vanilla}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_comparison} summarizes all methods evaluated on Qwen3-8B.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO removes $\\dsyc$ entirely and relies on the NLI-based behavioral reward alone.\nIf the failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} stemmed solely from the representation reward, this ablation should recover calibration quality.\nIt does not.\nThe $\\sycon$ trajectory for $\\rthree$-only GRPO oscillates without sustained improvement.\nSeed 1 peaks at 0.153 (checkpoint 6500) and ends at 0.143 (checkpoint 8000); seed 2 peaks at 0.143 (checkpoint 4000) and regresses to 0.095 by checkpoint 6000.\nOnly 4 of 26 checkpoints across both seeds exceed vanilla (15.4\\%), while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing; Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nBoth seeds exhibit oscillations of similar amplitude ($\\Delta \\approx 0.069$) and identical crash depth (0.074), but crash onset differs by ${\\sim}2{,}500$ steps, indicating stochastic drift in a saturated reward landscape.\nCross-seed rank correlation is ${\\approx}1.0$ during early training (steps 1000--3000) but drops to $\\rho{\\approx}0.33$ (n.s.) after step 4000, with spike timing and magnitude diverging---consistent with method-inherent warm-up followed by stochastic divergence.\nActivation analysis confirms the drift is not representational: the $\\dsyc$ direction at layer 19 yields nearly identical AUROC across consecutive checkpoints ($\\Delta < 0.01$), and $\\rthree$ reward saturates at ${\\sim}0.98$ (Welch $t$-test $p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ swings by up to 17\\%.\nGRPO drifts in a flat reward landscape while representations remain stable, reinforcing the disconnect between discriminability and behavioral utility.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO exhibits the same stubbornness pattern as the $\\dsyc$ approaches (high $\\nof$, near-zero $\\tof$) at milder severity: the sparse scalar reward per completion cannot teach the model \\emph{which} belief changes are warranted.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\sycon$ trajectories for $\\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (seed 1; ACT seed 1 exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (16/16); $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/26 checkpoints across seeds (15.4\\%). Both GRPO seeds show the same oscillation pattern with stochastic timing.}\n\\label{fig:trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Observational comparison: ACT vs.\\ GRPO.}\nACT~\\citep{consistency2025bctact} achieves $\\sycon = 0.301$ at checkpoint 8000, more than $2{\\times}$ the vanilla baseline and above every scalar RL and inference-time variant (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}).\nThe ACT trajectory is not monotonic: $\\sycon = 0.255$ at checkpoint 2000, dips to 0.214 at checkpoint 4000 ($-4.1$ percentage points), recovers to 0.262 at checkpoint 6000, and reaches 0.301 at checkpoint 8000.\nACT also experiences stubbornness episodes---the checkpoint-4000 dip is consistent with transient competition between the SFT and L2 consistency loss objectives---yet it self-recovers by checkpoint 6000, unlike GRPO where stubbornness persists (Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nActivation analysis shows a correlated asymmetry: ACT's $\\dsyc$ discriminability recovers during the $\\sycon$ dip (AUROC $+0.013$), whereas GRPO's continues declining ($-0.004$).\nACT and GRPO differ along multiple dimensions---supervision granularity (per-token vs.\\ per-completion), loss family (cross-entropy + L2 vs.\\ REINFORCE), training data format (labeled pairs vs.\\ sampled completions), optimization dynamics (SFT convergence vs.\\ RL exploration), and LoRA adapter capacity (ACT targets 7 module types with ${\\sim}1.75{\\times}$ more trainable parameters vs.\\ GRPO's 4)---so the performance gap cannot be attributed to any single factor without controlled ablation.\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} successfully reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking, where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients.\nTogether with our results, this suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\\rthree$-only GRPO), consistent with per-completion information compression as an independent factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Scalar reward limitation (observational).}\nAcross all GRPO configurations in our experiments, every scalar per-completion reward---whether $\\dsyc$-based or purely behavioral---failed to achieve reliable sycophancy calibration, while ACT's dense per-token supervision succeeded ($\\sycon = 0.301$).\nThis observation is consistent with supervision granularity as a relevant factor, but remains correlational: ACT and GRPO differ along five dimensions (see above), and isolating individual contributions requires controlled ablation.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nCondition-aware rewards that jointly encode belief-change direction and evidential warrant, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and process reward models with per-step feedback each offer paths forward.\nThe scalar per-completion reward structure is shared by PPO, RLOO, and other REINFORCE-style methods; whether they exhibit similar failures remains an open question.\nBeyond sycophancy, the principle that discriminability does not guarantee scalar reward utility may apply to other behavioral attributes where the target and its complement share the same geometric direction~\\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n",

    "oldString": "GRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} ranks sampled completions by reward and updates the policy toward higher-reward outputs.\nWe define $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rone$ is a direction-based component and $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\nThree formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into $\\rone$:",
    "newString": "Three formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into a direction-based signal $\\rone$.\nFor F1 and F3, $\\rone$ enters as part of a GRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} reward $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\nF2 incorporates $\\rone$ into a custom SFT loss instead (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}):",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Framework}\n\\label{sec:framework}\n\n\\subsection{Direction Extraction}\n\\label{ssec:extraction}\n\nWe extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure).\nWe construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.\nStyle matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\\%$, shared opening phrase ${\\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.\nEvidence strength is annotated at three levels: \\emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \\emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \\emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data).\nFrom the 181 paired examples (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:dsyc}\n\\dsyc = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{N}\\left(\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \\mathbf{h}^{-}_i\\right)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.\nThe target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$).\n\n\\subsection{Reward Formulations}\n\\label{ssec:reward}\n\nGRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} ranks sampled completions by reward and updates the policy toward higher-reward outputs.\nWe define $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rone$ is a direction-based component and $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\nThree formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into $\\rone$:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item[\\textbf{F1}] \\textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\\times}$ scaled cosine. See \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.\n\\item[\\textbf{F2}] \\textbf{Binary probe.} $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients.\n\\item[\\textbf{F3}] \\textbf{Continuous probe.} $\\rone = 1 - P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.\n\\end{itemize}\nThe weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration rather than grid search (Appendix~\\ref{app:training}).\nAs an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.\n\n\\subsection{Online Recalibration}\n\\label{ssec:recalib}\n\nLoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\\dsyc$ every 500 steps.\nThe cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\\approx}\\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.\n\n\\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}\n\\label{ssec:baselines}\n\nWe implement Activation Consistency Training \\citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.\nUnlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\\label{ssec:eval}\n\nThe \\emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \\emph{No-Flip rate} ($\\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.\nTheir harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:sycon}\n\\sycon = \\frac{2 \\cdot \\tof \\cdot \\nof}{\\tof + \\nof}\n\\end{equation}\nAnalogous to the $F_1$ score, the harmonic mean penalizes methods that achieve high performance on one metric while failing on the other---refusing all corrections (low $\\tof$, high $\\nof$) or capitulating universally (high $\\tof$, low $\\nof$).\nArithmetic mean results show qualitatively similar patterns and are reported in supplementary materials.\nFlip classification uses \\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base} (MNLI mismatched accuracy 90.0\\%, SNLI test 92.4\\%; \\citealt{he2023debertav3}) for stance classification, following \\citet{consistency2025bctact}.\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nGRPO uses a group size of 2 due to memory constraints with 8B-parameter models, below the typical range of 8--64 \\citep{shao2024grpo}; full hyperparameters are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:training}.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3), plus $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.\n",

the \\emph{No-Flip rate} ($\\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.\nTheir harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:sycon}\n\\sycon = \\frac{2 \\cdot \\tof \\cdot \\nof}{\\tof + \\nof}\n\\end{equation}\nAnalogous to the $F_1$ score, the harmonic mean penalizes methods that achieve high performance on one metric while failing on the other---refusing all corrections (low $\\tof$, high $\\nof$) or capitulating universally (high $\\tof$, low $\\nof$).\nArithmetic mean results show qualitatively similar patterns and are reported in supplementary materials.\nFlip classification uses \\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base} (MNLI mismatched accuracy 90.0\\%, SNLI test 92.4\\%; \\citealt{he2023debertav3}) for stance classification, following \\citet{consistency2025bctact}.\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nGRPO uses a group size of 2 due to memory constraints with 8B-parameter models, below the typical range of 8--64 \\citep{shao2024grpo}; full hyperparameters are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:training}.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3), plus $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.\n",

See \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.",
