    "oldString": "We show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\n\n\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}",
    "newString": "We show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\nBoth probe experiments (exp-008, exp-008v3) use a custom SFT training loop that combines the standard language modeling loss with probe and behavioral penalties, rather than GRPO; the probe trap is therefore independent of GRPO's optimization dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{The Probe Reward Trap}\n\\label{sec:probe_trap}\n\nThe cosine formulations fail because the continuous projection of $\\dsyc$ produces a gap too narrow for reward contrast.\nA natural remedy is to discretize: train a logistic probe on $\\dsyc$ and threshold its output, yielding $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \\S\\ref{ssec:reward}).\nWe show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\n\n\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}\nWithout stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy discovers within ${\\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\\tof = 0.978$, $\\nof = 0.006$).\nThis motivates the frozen-probe design below.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}\nWith stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.\nBy step 500, $\\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\\sycon = 0$.\nQualitative analysis reveals distinct failure modes: \\emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24\\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \\emph{deliberation traps} (29\\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; and evidence-graded hedging, where the model adjusts its refusal style rather than its acceptance rate (Appendix~\\ref{app:taxonomy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}\n\\label{tab:probe_trajectory}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nStep & $\\tof$ & $\\nof$ & $\\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\\\\n\\midrule\n500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\\\\n1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\\\\n1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\\\\n2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}\n\\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}\n\\label{fig:probe_trap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.\nAt step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000---well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy---and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\\rone$ and $\\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.\nEntropy does not collapse until step ${\\sim}1840$.\nThis gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.\nDetecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.\n\n\\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}\nThe differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.\nWith gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\\dsyc$.\nAny reward conditioned only on $\\dsyc$---continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen---will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.\nEscaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\\dsyc$ does not encode~\\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.\n",

    "originalFile": "Representation engineering shows that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in activation space. We extract a contrastive sycophancy direction ($\\dsyc$) from style-controlled pairs, achieving cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B. In experiments on Qwen3-8B, we test three $\\dsyc$-based reward formulations with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO); every formulation failed. The cosine reward could not separate valid revision from sycophantic capitulation in the narrow online activation gap. The probe reward created a stubbornness trap: the true-positive flip rate dropped to zero within 500 steps while entropy remained healthy. Removing $\\dsyc$ entirely and training with a purely behavioral reward also failed: only 4 of 26 checkpoints across two seeds exceeded vanilla, while supervised fine-tuning exceeded vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 4/4 ongoing). The root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility: $\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}. A single scalar reward per completion could not convey which belief changes were warranted in our setting; dense per-token supervision (ACT) succeeded, though the methods differ along multiple dimensions beyond supervision granularity alone.\n",

    "oldString": "AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$) at its best layer (L19).",
    "newString": "AUROC $0.827$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI $[0.795,\\, 0.857]$) at its best layer (L19).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Direction Discriminability}\n\\label{sec:direction_quality}\n\nBefore evaluating $\\dsyc$ as a reward signal (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), we first establish that it captures a genuine sycophancy-related signal in the residual stream.\nIf the direction failed to discriminate sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations, downstream reward failures would be trivially explained by poor direction quality rather than by a fundamental gap between discriminability and reward utility.\nWe evaluate discriminability along five axes: cross-validated classification accuracy, evidence-strength stratification, cross-model transfer, training stability, and probe ablation.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:direction_quality} summarizes the per-layer AUROC profiles for both models.\n\n\\paragraph{Qwen3-8B layer-wise discriminability.}\nWe evaluate $\\dsyc$ via 5-fold cross-validated AUROC on 181 style-controlled contrastive pairs (\\S\\ref{ssec:extraction}).\nThe direction achieves AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$) at its best layer (L19).\nDiscriminability emerges gradually across depth: layers L0--L8 perform near chance, statistical significance ($p < 0.01$) first appears at L16, and performance peaks at L19.\nThis gradual emergence, with the peak in the upper third of the network, suggests that sycophancy-relevant information is encoded as a late-stage representation rather than an input-level confound.\nThe direction also captures evidential granularity.\nAUROC rises monotonically from 0.675 on weak-evidence pairs to 0.883 on medium and 0.929 on strong pairs.\nThis gradient indicates that $\\dsyc$ encodes the degree of evidential warrant, not merely a binary sycophancy label.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}\nTo rule out that these results are specific to Qwen3-8B's representation geometry, we repeat the full extraction and evaluation pipeline on Llama-3-8B-Instruct.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares the two models.\nLlama-3-8B achieves CV AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$) at L16, with a parallel evidence-strength gradient (weak 0.711, medium 0.864, strong 0.911).\nThe best-layer depth differs (L16 vs.\\ L19), consistent with the architectural differences between the two families, but the qualitative pattern is identical: gradual emergence, best-layer selection in the upper third, and monotonic strength scaling.\nDiscriminability is robust across knowledge domains, with per-domain AUROCs on Llama-3-8B ranging from 0.751 (geography) to 0.844 (commonsense).\nWhen surface confounds (response length, hedging patterns) are explicitly controlled, Llama-3-8B's AUROC rises to 0.923, which confirms that the direction captures semantic content rather than stylistic artifacts.\nThe consistent results across architecturally distinct model families indicate that contrastive sycophancy directions reflect a shared representational phenomenon rather than a model-specific idiosyncrasy.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model $\\dsyc$ discriminability. CV AUROC is 5-fold cross-validated; evidence-strength AUROCs are evaluated on the full dataset at each model's best layer.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nModel & Best Layer & CV AUROC & Weak & Medium & Strong \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-8B & L19 & $0.827{\\pm}0.028$ & 0.675 & 0.883 & 0.929 \\\\\nLlama-3-8B & L16 & $0.779{\\pm}0.019$ & 0.711 & 0.864 & 0.911 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_auroc.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer AUROC of $\\dsyc$ as a function of normalized depth (layer index / total layers). Qwen3-8B (36 layers, solid) shows gradual emergence from near-chance at L0 to a peak at L19, with performance plateauing in the final third. Llama-3-8B (32 layers, dashed; layers 16--31 only) exhibits a similar upper-third concentration. Both models achieve best discriminability in layers 50--60\\% through the network.}\n\\label{fig:direction_quality}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Training stability and probe ablation.}\nTwo additional analyses confirm the robustness of $\\dsyc$ under training dynamics.\nFirst, we track direction drift during LoRA fine-tuning: at step 500, the cosine similarity between the original and re-extracted $\\dsyc$ is approximately 0.96, and per-layer AUROC remains above 0.77.\nThe direction drifts slowly relative to the 500-step recalibration interval (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), so the reward signal does not become stale between updates.\nSecond, we compare $\\dsyc$ against a random baseline: a logistic probe trained on the learned direction achieves AUROC 0.835, versus 0.727 for a probe trained on a random direction of equal dimensionality ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.108$).\nThe learned direction carries substantially more sycophancy-relevant information than an arbitrary axis in the same activation space.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for reward design.}\nThese results establish that $\\dsyc$ reliably separates sycophantic from non-sycophantic representations across models, evidence-strength levels, and training checkpoints.\nDirection quality is not the bottleneck.\nThe reward failures documented in \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap} therefore cannot be attributed to a noisy or degenerate direction; they point to a deeper incompatibility between discriminability and reward utility.\n",

    "newString": "$^d$Below typical 8--16; not tuned.\n$^e$Binary probe (F2) uses a custom SFT training loop rather than GRPO; group size is N/A. LoRA and optimizer settings match the GRPO column.\n\\end{table}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Training Details}\n\\label{app:training}\n\n\\paragraph{Reward weight selection.}\nThe weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration: $w_1{=}0.25$ was initially chosen to down-weight the noisy cosine reward term; this was later increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ for F1d after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed the cosine term to contribute only ${\\sim}8.3\\%$ of total reward variance.\nThe empirical scaling factor in F1a ($\\dsyc/20$) is a heuristic normalization; we did not find it critical to the failure modes reported.\n\n\\paragraph{Response truncation.}\nAll GRPO experiments use \\texttt{max\\_completion\\_length}=256, resulting in 80--84\\% truncation of multi-turn responses.\nThis setting is shared across all conditions and does not affect relative comparisons; we retain it because response-level decisions typically manifest within the first 100--200 tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:hyperparams} compares training configurations for GRPO variants and ACT.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Training hyperparameters. All experiments use Qwen3-8B, DeepSpeed ZeRO-2, bf16 precision.}\n\\label{tab:hyperparams}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n& GRPO (F1, F3)$^e$ & ACT \\\\\n\\midrule\nLearning rate & 5e-5 & 5e-5 \\\\\nEffective batch & 16 & 16 \\\\\nMax steps & 8{,}000 & 8{,}000 \\\\\nWarmup steps & 200 & 200 \\\\\nLR schedule & linear$^a$ & linear \\\\\nGrad.\\ clipping & --- & 1.0 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLoRA rank & 64 & 64 \\\\\nLoRA $\\alpha$ & 64 & 128 \\\\\nLoRA modules & 4$^b$ & 7$^c$ \\\\\nLoRA dropout & 0 & 0.05 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGroup size & 2$^d$ & --- \\\\\nKL $\\beta$ & 0.04 & --- \\\\\nMax compl.\\ len. & 256 & --- \\\\\nMax seq.\\ len. & --- & 1{,}024 \\\\\nL2 loss weight & --- & 0.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\\\[2pt]\n\\raggedright\\footnotesize\n$^a$Cosine schedule for $\\rthree$-only.\n$^b$q/k/v/o\\_proj.\n$^c$+ gate/up/down\\_proj (${\\sim}1.75{\\times}$ params; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n$^d$Below typical 8--16; not tuned.\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO reward variants.}\nCosine $\\rone$ (F1d): $w_1{=}0.40$, scale$=$5.0, $w_3{=}0.60$; reward computed at layers [19, 20, 24, 27, 32, 33, 35]; recalibration every 500 steps (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}).\n$\\rthree$-only: $w_3{=}1.0$; NLI model \\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}.\nF1a--F1c weight configurations are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:cosine_chain}.\n\n\\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}\n\\label{app:taxonomy}\n\nManual inspection of 100 probe-trained responses (exp-008v3, step 2000) reveals three dominant failure modes.\n\\emph{Sycophantic stubbornness} (24\\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.\n\\emph{Deliberation traps} (29\\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.\n\\emph{Evidence-graded hedging}: under weak evidence, responses show surface compliance without substantive change; under strong evidence, responses add explicit hedging (``this is debatable,'' ``there are multiple perspectives'').\nThe model learns to modulate its \\emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \\emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.\n",
